Mr. Philltps:
- Mr. Dunn:
Tokyo's despatch No. 1067, dated Novem-
ber 28, 1934:
This despatch gives an account of the
offorts and determination of the Japanese to
develop a Japanese automobile industry and to
offoct an automobile sales monopoly on the
order of the petroleum control plan.  It is
recommended that the despatch be read in whole.
A draft plan for a national motor-car
industry polioy is being considered, which
will control the manufacture and assembly of
motor-cars; develop a stendard Japanese motor-
car; subsidize domestic manufacture; possibly
raise import duties on foreign automobiles
and parts; and require the use of Japanese
cars by government offices.
A report from Mukden states that the
"Manchukuo" Government is considering a
monopoly on automobile sales, which would
reserve the Manchurian market for the Japanese
industry.
American automobile assembly plants in
Japan have seen the hand-writing on the wall,
and

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.94)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2-
and are endeavoring to associate themselves
with Japanese interests. Their efforts
are meeting with opposition from the Jana-
nese authorities.
American manufacturers believe that
the Japanese will not be able to develop
an automobile industry that can compete
with American products. The Embassy does
not entirely agree

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to ref:r t, che recent correspondence
JAN2
between this Embassy and the Department on the subject of
poly Law in "Manchukuo" and to report that & somewhat similar
system for the control of the automobile inuustry and trade
in Japan and "Manchukuo" appears to be gradually aeveloping.
Several months ago the War Department, the Department
of Commerce and Industry and other interested branches of
the

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.96)
一-
he Japanese Government org:nized a committee to investi-
gate and report on the est blishment of a national policy
in regard to the motor-car industry. In August last this
committee appointed a sub-co mittee to formulate a draft
plan for the encouragement nd :ssistance of the domestic
manufacture of automobiles. The araft plan, accordin to
the vernacular newspt ers, consists of the licensing of the
manufacturing or assembling of motor-curs, the develooment
of a standard car to be caileo the "l w Economic Car" vhich
will be built oy the existing Januuese manufacturers, the
subsidizing of aomestic manufacturers, the possible raising
of import cuties on automobile narts, ana the encouragement
of the use by all Government of ici: 1s nd public bodics of
the "New Economic Car".
The Department's attention is lso invited to Vice
83.7'71
(onsul Edson's r port of July :5, 19:4, from Mukden, en-
titled "Manufacture o Automobile Chassis in ukden", in
which it is stated that & company numed the "Towa Jidosha
Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha" (in Chinese the "T'ung Ho" auto-
mobile industrial company) had beer formed to assemble
motor-cars from Jupanese perts at the former Trench ortar
Arsenal at Mukden. Some 96 or 97 por cent of the capital
stock of the company, which was formed in accoroance with
the provisions of an Imperial Ordinance of "nchukuo",
is owned by Japanese interests, including the South Man-
churia Railway and several Japanese automobile manufacturing
concerns. In the latter part of the report referred to
Mr. Edson states that rumors are current to the effect that
the

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
the "Manchukuo" government is considering the possibility
of establishing an automobile sales monopoly in Manchuria.
An analysis of these two plans (the licensing system
in Japan and the monopoly system in manchuria) reveals a
striking resenblance to the petroleum control systems
which have recently been apnlied to the tvo countries.
The general scheme, as in the case of petroleum, apparent-
ly is to develop a strong Jananese incustry and to reserve
the manchurian market for this inaustry. This is to be
accomplished, it would appear, by oling cown the American
assembling plants in Janan (Ford ind General otors) by
means of : licensing system, &t the :a time builcing up
a Japanese automobile industry by designing a standard car
which can compete vith the Fora ano Chevrolet, by means of
subsicies tnd higher tariffs, na by copelling the use of
the standard Japanese car by J panere goverrmental org ni-
zations. The manufacture of trucks suited to Japanese con-
ditions will be encouraged in the same way. The cars and
trucks, in parts, will be shi pea to ukon, here they will
be assemulea by the Jananese-controllea Towa Jiaosha Kogyo
Kabushiki kaisha. In orcer to prevent strong competition
from American cars and trucks, & s&les monopoly will be
established, preference being iven by the monopoly to
the cars assembled in "Manchukuo" by the Japanese-controlled
company, and the American cars being put on a quota basis.
This is practically the general scheme adoptea for the
Japanese petroleum industry, except that the anchuria Oil
Company (correspond.ng to the Towa Jidosha Kogyo Kaisha)
does not use Japanese materials but refines imported crude
oi1s.
It

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
It cannot be statec positively that the scheme
as outlined above is exactly the plan which will be
workea out eventually, but there are various inaica-
tions that the Japanese authorities have some such
scheme under consideration.
Various steps, both positive anu negative, have
already been taken toward the fulfillment of the above
or some similar plan. The positive steps consist prin-
cipally of the organization ut Mukden of the Towa Jidosha
Kogyo K bushiki Kaisha, as described above, and the orga-
nization in Japan of the Niss un Jidosha Kubushiki Naisha.
Some months ago an attempt was made to amalgamate the
six or seven existing Japanese automobile manufacturing
companies into one strong company, able to compete with
the American companies. The amalgamation did not matericl-
ize, but two of the largest of the manufacturers, the
Ishikawajima Works and the Dat Automobile Company, amal-
gamated unuer the aegis of the Nippon S ngyo K: bushiki
Kaisha (Kuhara interests) and formeo the Nissan Jidosha
Kabushiki Kaisha. This company has erected a large,
modern automobile manufacturing plant near Yokohama,
completely equipped With American machinery and under
the management of American automotive engineers. It is
at present manufacturing a small car, somewhat like the
"Baby Austin", in fairly large quantities, and is prepared
to manufacture parts in very large quantities. It is
probable that this factory will be the basis of the forth-
coming scheme for Japanese control of the automobile trade
of Japan and Manchuria.
Another positive step toward the realization of the
Japanese

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
apanese plan consisted of the recent change in the
import tariff of "Manchukuo", whereby the duty on complete
trucks and chassis was rcis  to 30 ner cent ad valorem
and the duty on automotive parts was lowereu to 10 per cent
ad valorem. This change woulc not rfect American auto-
mobile distributors were it possible for them to take ad-
vantage of the lower duty on purts and assemble their
cars and trucks in "lanchuiuo". As will b explained
later, however, it aopears tht this step on the part of the
American distributors will meet the o) osition of the Jana-
nese authorities.
The negative steps which have been taken by the Jana-
nese authorities in realization of their nlan of control
over the automobile inaustry of Janan ana .anchuria co sist
of actions which tena to o ce furtner develooment of the
American automobile trade in J.pan and Manchuria. The
General Motors Cor oration h : for s me years had a Japan
subsidiary assembling olant, opratec as General motors
Japan, Limited. Recently the Corporation, reading the hand-
writing on the wall, decided that the nart of wisdom would
be to associate itself  th some Japanese interest and thereby
obtain the partial ranking of a Japanese automobile manu-
facturing concern. It also olanned to have the Japanese
interest manufacture some of the parts used in the assembling
of the cars, in order to attain further ranking as a Japa-
nese manufacturing firm. It arranged, therefore, to sell
one-half of the stock of General Motors Japan, Limited, to
the Nippon Sangyo Kabushiki Kaisha (which also controls the
Nissan Jidosha Kabushiki Kaisha), which would use its factory
near

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
near Yokohama to supply Chevrolet parts to General Motors
Japan, Limited. The deal, ho" ver, wes blocked by the
op osition of the military , Who, accord ng to Mr. Kurusu,
the Chief of the Comercial Affairs Bureau of the Foreign
Office, want an "all-Japan" a tonobile industry. The trans-
action was blocked by i refusal to permit the General Motors
interests to export from Japan the funds which it vould re-
ceive from the sale of the shares of General Motors Japan,
Limited. At the time of writing the deal is still hanging
fire.
The Ford Motor Company, lso realizing the future trend
of the automobile trade in Japan, ishes to change its assembling
plant in Japan to a mmanufacturing lant, in order to produce
within Japan as many of the necessary parts as ossible and
thereby acguire the ranking of a Japanese manufacturing concern.
The Company began negotiations for land fo: its manufacturing
plant, but the owner of the lano was anpro chec by an Army
officer, who aavisec hin not to sell to the foreign firm.
While this has not blocked the Company's lans entirely, it
has caused them to hesitate nd make other inquiries before
proceeding with further investment in Japan, especially in view
of the fact that a licensing system ray be imposeu upon them.
Several months ago the agents in Japan and ...anchuria of
Chrysler cars ( including Chrysler, Dodge, DeSoto and Plymouth)
organized a firm to assemble these cars at Dairen. Permission
to erect an assembling plant was obtained from the Government
of the Kwantung Leased Territory and c nstruction of the plant
was begun when the Government was compelled, upon instructions
of the Kwantung Army, to cancel the license for the plant.
The

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.97)
- 7 -
The explanation of the Army was that it did not wish to
have a private concern in Dairen manufacturing or assem-
bling automobiles in competition with the Towa Jidosha
Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha (the comp:ny org nized to assemble
cars from Japanese parts in Mukden). (D:iren's renort to
the Embassy of August 1, 194, & copy of which was sent to
the Department). The Army arrangea to copensate the agents
for the funds already invested in the assembling lant, but
the fact remains that the agents of American cars re not
being permitted to assemble their cars in anchuria, because
such assembling will interfere ith the Japanese Army's pro-
oosal to establish a J:panese notor-car inaustry in Japan
and in menchuria.
Some of the merican motor-car  nuf: cturers' repre-
sentatives in Japan co not believe that the Japanese au-
thorities will be able to acco lish their orogram tnd to
arive out American autorobiles or even seriously to cur-
tail their production in this country. Vith the limited
arket afforued by this country and -anc uria, they assert,
the Japanese will not be able to produce in suf icient cuan-
t:ties to enable them to tnuficture at a price to compete
v. th Fora or Chevrolet, and moreover, lacking the incentive
to progress occasionea by the strong competition in the
United States and Europe, they will always have a car l cking
the latest improvements, while the Japanese market has
been trained to expect and want the latest designs. The
Embassy, however, does not entirely agree with this view.
Experience in other industries indicates that the Japanese
can produce practically anything at competitive prices when
they

nationalism and tremendous powers of organization may
carry even a project such as that outlined above to

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 12, 1935.
deM:
Ge..c.... m.m.N
Please note a memorandum (attached) of
conversation with a representative of the
Ford Motor Company.
I consulted Mr. Ward this morning in
regard to the question whether American
treaty rights would be violated by the
enactment of legislation to control the
automobile industry in Japan. It was
Mr. Ward's view, in the light of information
now available, that there would be no basis
for representations to the Japanese Govern-
ment in regard to the proposed bill referred
to in the Embassy's telegram 165 of August 10,
noon, for the reason that the second para-
graph of Article 7 of the Treaty of Commerce
stipulates that the question whether a
company organized in one country shall be
permitted to transact its business or
industry in the other is to be determined
according "to the laws and regulations en-
acted or established in the respective
countries or in any part thereof".
He pointed out, however, that a different
question would be raised if the conditions
imposed by the law apon the Ford Company
were such as to make it impossible for the
Company to operate profitably in Japan.
Mr. Ward

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Mr. Ward and I were in agreement in
believing that no action of any kind should
be taken until more information is available
in regard to the proposed law.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Al
August 9, 1935.
1935 A16 16 4 2 59
CONVERSATION:
Mr. Longley
Counseu for:the Ford Motor Company.
Droartinent of State
Mr. Dooman.
894.797
Subject:
Future Developments in Japan as They
May Affect the Automobile Industry.
Mr. Longley stated that the Ford interests in Japan
began negotiating some time ago for the purchase of a new
tract of land in Japan with a view to the enlarging of
894.797/7
their plant; that some time after they began to feel
forces at work to prevent the successful conclusion of
the negotiations; but that recently the purchase had been
consummated. There were, he said, good reasons to believe
that the Japanese army was opposed to the expansion of
American automobile factories in Japan, and the Ford
Company entertained doubts as to the wisdom of carrying
out the plans to expand its plent in Japan. Mr. Longley
FILED
further stated that the Ford manager in Japan had recently
informed his principals in the United States that a bill
would probably be introduced into the Diet at the next
session for the purpose of regulating the automobile
F/F
industry -- probably to institute some system of licensing.
F
The manager had recommended that the plans for expansion
G
be completed before the bill is enacted, as he expressed
confidence

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
confidence that vested interests "could be protected".
Mr. Longley asked whether the Department had any information
in regard to "the situation in Japan" which would be helpful
to the Company in making its decision in regard to the
question whether or not the plant in Japan should be expand-
ed at this time.
I told Mr. Longley that, so far as I knew, no report
had been received from our officers in Japan in regard to
anactueut
industry. I suggested that if the Ford manager in Japan
approached the Embassy, the Embassy would, I felt certain,
cooperate in every appropriate manner.
Speaking entirely for myself, I expressed the opinion
that the Ford manager in Japan seemed somewhat optimistic.
I asked Mr. Iongley whether he were familiar with the Japa-
nese law for the regulation of the oil industry. Mr. Longley
said that he was familiar with it in a general way. I then
stated that when a law applicable to natives as well as to
foreigners is enacted by a country, and when, by reason of
the circumstances involved, such law operates adversely
against foreign interests and to the advantage of native
interests, the protection of the foreign interests involved
is never an easy matter. In certain cases it was practicable
to remonstrate with the foreign government concerned only on
the basis of equity against the application of such law;
and

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
and that when equity was invoked the government enacting
such law would usually fall back on the argument that
national interests were paramount to private interests.
I reminded Mr. Longley that I was speaking only in a
general way, that I knew nothing of the proposed Japanese
legislation, and that I could not assure him that the con-
fidence of the manager in Japan that vested rights could
be protected was warranted until we knew something more
of what the Japanese had in mind.
Mr. Iongley stated that he had come merely to discuss
the matter informally, and that he was glad to obtain the
expression of my private views, as these views were sub-
stantially those held by officers in the United States of
the Ford Company.
There then ensued a brief discussion in regard to the
advantages and disadvantages of permitting Japanese financial
interests to invest in American industrial enterprises in
Japan. I remarked that it was an interesting fact that
those enterprises with which Japanese were not associated
were encountering difficulties, whereas enterprises operated
both by Americans and Japanese seem to be having smooth
sailing. I referred to the fact that a substantial if not
a majority interest in the Ford enterprise in England was
British, and I wondered why the Ford Company had not in-
vited Japanese to become associated with the Ford plant in
Japan

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
Japan, where, as everyone knew, intensely nationalistic
feelings prevail. Mr. Longley stated that he did not
know the reasons for pursuing such a policy in Japan, but
he assumed that it was due to certain technical reasons.
He mentioned the fact that General Motors endeavored to
interest Japanese in their plant in Japan, but without
success.
As Mr. Longley was about to leave, I stressed the fact
that any statements made by me were not to be construed in
any wayas representing the views of the Department.
Mr. Longley stated that he realized the conversation was
purely informal.

EPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 30, 1935.
Tokyo's despatch No. 143l of August 10,
1935, transmits press items in regard to
the contemplated bill for the control of
the automobile industry.
In the light of statements made by
the Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the
Foreign Office to Neville (see enclosure
No. 2, despatch No. 1422 of August 8, 1935,
attached) it is evident that decision has
been taken by the military authorities to
develop in Japan an automobile industry
independent of the plants already estab-
lished there by foreign (American) in-
terests. It is reported by a Japanese
newspaper that (enclosure No. 2 to
despatch No. 1431) only those companies
in which a majority of the shares are
held by Japanese subjects will be licensed
hereafter to engage in the manufacture of
automobiles. It is reported that companies
now engaged in the manufacture of auto-
mobiles, but the majority of whose shares
are not owned by Japanese subjects, will
be permitted to continue operation, but
that "new installations by old companies
will not be recognized retroactively".
Assuming that this press report is correct,
it is apparently intended that Ford and
General

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
IVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
General Motors will be permitted to con-
tinue in the manufacturing and assembling
of motor cars at the present volume, but
that they will not be permitted to expand.
This information is somewhat reassuring
in view of earlier press reports to the
effect that Ford and General Motors would
not be permitted to operate at all unless
Japanese were given a majority share in
their respective enterprises.

a draft bill to control the automobile industry in
Japan by establishing a license system, under which
foreign automobile firms cannot operate in Japan
unless Japanese are admitted to owmership of a
majority of the stock, yesterday received the approval
of the Cabinet and, according to present plans, will

to

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)

894.00/550
to its despatch No. 1422 of August 8, 1935, on the
subject of nationalist tendencies in Japanese in-
dustry.
This information is based upon reports in the
Japanese and English-language press which were pub-
lished this morning. Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau
of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, confirms
that the newspaper reports are authentic. The Embassy
desires to include this matter in today's pouch, al-
though a thorough treatment of the subject must await
further official information. The Department will
perhaps be interested to know that Mr. Kurusu has
stated to me in conversation, that this plan has been
the subject of lengthy discussions; that it is primarily
a military project, and that its acceptance now by the
Government as a project of legislation is due to fear
of the Ford enterprises. The measure, I am informed,
will not affect automobile plants actually in operation
at the present time, but will prevent additions or al-
terations to foreign controlled automobile plants in Japan.

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)

No 1431 of Aug. 10, 1935 from the
Embassy at Tokyo:
COPY
THE OKUYAMA SERVICE
Saturday, August 10, 1935.
Foreign Auto Companies to Be Affected by New Law;
How Will This Great Whirlpool Be Settled?
Asahi Shimbun, August 10.
As reported in the yesterday evening edition of the
Asahi, the automobile industry control bill is to be
presented to the next session of the Diet, opening in
December, for enactment, giving the first indication
of a time in the future when the big flood of foreign-
made automobiles in Japan will be replaced by Japanese-
made cars.
Because the enactment of the law is for the pur-
pose of economic strengthening of the automobile
industry in Japan, it is naturally expected that there
will occur a certain degree of friction between the
vested rights of the foreign automobile companies
doing business in Japan and the law.
What tactics will the two greatest foreign
automobile companies, Ford and General Motors, employ
to cope with the new situation?
The automobile market in Japan at present is being
actually controlled by these two automobile companies
supplying the Ford and Chevrolet cars, respectively,
the types of cars which dominate the market in Japan.
The Second Item of the Automobile Industry Control
Bill says:
"The Applicants for licenses must be joint-
stock companies in which more than half the stock
is held by Japanese subjects or by juridical person
or persons established in accordance with the laws
of the Japanese Empire."
Thus foreign capital is clearly refused. Some more
trouble is foreseen, for the Ford assembling plant at
Yokohama is about to construct a large factory for the
purpose of manufacturing automobile parts in an effort
to grasp the hegemony of the Oriental market with Japan
as the center. Already the Ford Company, Japan, has
purchased for this purpose a land of 1l0,000 tsubo
(3,000 tsubo is equal to 2.5 acres) at the reclaimed
ground

=== Page 30 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
ground in Tsurumi between Tokyo and Yokohama at a cost
of several million yen.
Sewers along the street have been completed and work
for the erection of the plant is in progress.
The Automobile Industry Control Bill further states:
"The rights of companies that are already
established and fulfill the above requirements will
be safeguarded, but companies established after
the promulgation of the law or new installations
by old companies will not be recognized retro-
actively whenthe law goes into force."
This means that after August 9, 1935 there shall
be no new companies established or any expansion of old
companies.
What will the Ford Company do with the spacious land?
There are four conceivable courses open for
adoption by the Ford:
1. The Ford Company abandon the idea of erecting
the new plant and adheres to its present vested rights,
that is, concentrate on assembling of cars.
2. The company entirely withdraw from the Japanese
market and shift its headquarters to China or any other
Oriental country and thereby menace the Japanese in-
dustries.
3. The Company in accordance with the terms of the
Automobile Industry Control Law cede 5l per cent of the
stock to Japanese financial interests and hand over the
right of controlling the automobile industry to Japan.
4. The Company, considering that in view of the
political conditions in Japan actual enforcement of the
proposed law will be about two years hence, hasten to
establish the automobile parts manufacturing plant, get
as much profit as possible during the ensuing two years
ad thenwithdraw from Japan.
The number one course is hardly possible in view
of the nature of the development of the industry. Number
two course is doubtful of adoption as any businessman
will be reluctant to give up his vested rights so easily.
Number three course is also not likely to be adopted
because Henry Ford is a practical industrialist. This
is against Ford's traditional spirit. Ford will not be
moved only by a matter of profit. Number four course,
even if adopted, will leave only the vested rights after
a few years.
If the Ford company is to remain in Japan, the
company cannot but take the Number three course. If
the

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the company carries out the third course suggested
against Henry Ford's spirit, the company will remain
and attain development, but the right of control will
be turned over to the hands of the Japanese.
The Ford Company turns out 13,O00 cars a year.
How the company will overcome the present difficult
situation is being watched by all circles concerned.
The Ministry of Commerce and Industry yesterday
afternoon notified the General Motors Japan and the
Ford Company Japan of the contents of the Automobile
Industry Control Bill as decided by the Cabinet
yesterday.

en
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
22 1935
Sir:
GELLD
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1431, dated August 10, 1935, in regard to the proposed
legislation to place the automobile manufacturing in-
dustry of Japan under a system of control by means of
licenses. As this is a matter of considerable impor-
tance to American industry (the only large automobile
manufacturers operating in Japan at present being the
Ford Motor Company of Japan, Limited, and General Motors
Japan, Limited), the Embassy has carefully studied the
proposed legislation.
It

=== Page 39 === (confidence 1.00)
144
-2-
It appears that the determination of the auto-
mobile industry policy was reached by the Automobile
Industry Investigation Commission, composed of offi-
cials of the departments of Commerce and Industry, Army,
Overseas Affairs, Finance, Home Affairs and Railways.
The present policy was agreed upon after the Commission
had considered and rejected plans for the development
of a Japanese automobile industry by either (1) heavily
subsidizing Japanese automobile manufacturing firms,
or (2) attempting to purchase the Japanese plants of
foreign automobile manufacturers. The policy as finally
agreed upon was submitted by the Minister for Commerce
and Industry to the Cabinet on August 9, 1935, and was
approved. The legislation to effectuate the policy has
not yet been drafted, but it is the intention of the
Cabinet that the policy itself is to be effective from
the date of determination of the policy, i.e., August 9,
1935.
1/
There is enclosed a translation, made by the Em-
bassy, of the announcement of policy in regard to the
automobile industry and the essential points of the
proposed legislation, as sent to the automobile manu-
facturing companies by the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry.
The Embassy has carefully examined this announce-
ment but the exact purport of the proposed legislation
not being entirely clear, especially as regards the
position of the foreign automobile manufacturing firms
operating

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.98)
15
-3-
operating in Japan (actually only the two American
firms mentioned above), Mr. Dickover of the Embassy
interviewed Mr. Kurusu, the Chief of the Bureau of
Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, on the sub-
2/
ject. A memorandum of their conversation is enclosed.
From this it will be noted that the proposed legis-
lation is aimed primarily at the Ford Motor Company
of Japan, Limited; that it probably will permit the
American motor companies at present operating in Japan
to continue operating at the present rate without
yielding control to Japanese capital; that if the
American companies operating in Japan wish to expand
they will be compelled to yield control to the Japanese;
and that if any of the American companies do yield
control to the Japanese and expand their facilities to
such an extent as to oppress the infant Japanese auto-
mobile industry, the law, in Mr. Kurusu's opinion,
would lose its meaning.
The purport of the law, as far as the Embassy can
determine, is simply to force the foreign companies to
maintain their present status, without further expansion,
and to secure the natural increase in the demand for
automobiles for the Japanese motor manufacturers. There
are as yet no Japanese motor manufacturers capable of
supplying the deficit in the market which would be caused
by the restriction of the activities of the two American
automobile manufacturers (Datsun can hardly as yet be
called a serious competitor of Ford and Chevrolet), but
there

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
there is little doubt that Japanese manufacturers will
be able to rise to the occasion and be able to supply
the market before the proposed legislation comes into
effect, which may be one or two years from now.
The legislation, if and when it comes into effect,
would appear to leave but three alternatives for the two
American motor concerns, i.e., (1) to continue opera-
tions at their present rate without taking in Japanese
capital and yielding control to Japanese interests,
(2) to yield control to Japanese interests and expand
with the market in the future, or (3) to withdraw from
Japan, establish themselves elsewhere in the Far East,
and compete with the Japan-made cars by importing com-
pleted cars into Japan. The two large American concerns
appear to have adopted different alternatives. Accord-
ing to the newspapers, the Ford Motor Company announced
on August 15:
We have not yet had an opportunity of
making a thorough study of the proposed
automobile control law and therefore are
not in a position to make any comments
on same. We can state positively, however,
that our directors have arrived at the
irrevocable decision not to relinquish
full control of our company under any
circumstances whatever.
The newspapers nevertheless report that the Mitsubishi
Heavy Industries Company is interested in buying the
controlling interest of the Ford Motor Company of Japan.
General Motors Japan, Limited, for some time past
has been eonsidering the sale of a part of its shares
to the Nippon Sangyo Kaisha (Kuhara interests), retaining
control

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
control, however, of the Company and acquiring an
interest in the Nippon Sangyo Kaisha, which would make
parts for the Chevrolet car. The negotiations for the
deal failed some months ago, however, because of the
opposition of the Japanese military, who wished to
develop an entirely Japanese motor industry. The Em-
bassy has been informed that the Managing Director of
General Motors Japan, Limited, has again approached
the Nippon Sangyo Kaisha on the subject of the sale of
an interest in the Company, but the newspapers report
that the Nippon Sangyo Kaisha, realizing that the sale
would now be in the nature of a forced sale, wants
much better terms than were offered in the previous
negotiations. According to the CHUGAI SHOGYO (trans-
lated by the ADVERTISER), Mr. Aikawa, the President
of Nissan Automobile Company (a subsidiary of Nippon
Sangyo Kaisha and the manufacturers of Datsun cars)
stated as follows:
If General Motors wants the same terms
as last year, the deal will have to be
dropped. It was necessary for Nissan
Automobile to accept the terms last year,
as the company had to sell its parts. Now
we have had a year's experience in making
parts and finished cars. We don't have to
be afraid to stick up for our own interests.
The automobile industry law seems likely
to be adopted by the Diet, and we must work
out a deal for cooperation based on the new
conditions."
The Embassy is not informed, however, whether or not
General Motors will consent to the sale of a controlling
interest in its Japanese subsidiary (the sale of a con-
trolling interest had not been considered in the previous
negotiations

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)
148
-6-
negotiations, according to the Managing Director of
the local firm). The Embassy is of the opinion, however,
that if either Ford or General Motors does sell a con-
trolling interest to Japanese, and thereby acquires
the right to expand and compete with the infant Japanese
industry, other legislation will be introduced to hold
the American firm in check.
As was stated by Mr. Kurusu in his conversation
with Mr. Dickover, the proposed legislation is sponsored
by the War Office. On August 9, when the announcement
of policy was made public, the War Office issued a state-
ment on the subject, which was translated by the OSAKA
MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI-NICHI of August 1O as follows:
It goes without saying that the motor
vehicle, from the standpoint of national
defense, is as important as the man and
horse. The use of motor vehicles in the
first line of military operations is
common knowledge today, but it may be
pointed out that the army, in case of war,
needs a large number of motor vehicles
for the transportation.
For the latter purpose, it is ex-
pected that ordinary motorcars will be
commandeered in time of emergency. And
moreover, the wartime demand for motor
vehicles has been rapidly increased in
recent years.
"Complete national defense, there-
fore, cannot be attained without develop-
ing domestic manufacture of motor vehicles.
"Unlike petroleum, for which Japan is
compelled to depend on foreign supplies due
to lack of ample resources in the country,
the development of the motor vehicle in-
dustry is merely a question of capital and
skill. Sufficient supply by the domestic
industry must be secured at all cost.
"With

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
山7
-7-
"With this view in mind, we are glad
to hear that the government has defined its
fundamental policy of encouraging an in-
dustry which has vital importance for the
national existence.
"The most important point in the present
bill is the provision that requires Japanese
management of every possible branch of the
motor vehicle industry. Although the Com-
merce Office will take charge of direct
supervision and inspection, as stipulated
in the present bill, the War Office is quite
willing to help the responsible authorities
when necessary. The War Office further
hopes that not only the government authori-
ties concerned, but also the general public
will spare no efforts to help in the estab-
lishment of the national nolicy and complete
national defense."
The proposed legislation has not attracted a
great amount of attention among the Japanese public.
Only a few of the Japanese newspapers have commented
editorially on the subject, and they have advised
caution in handling the matter, because of the inter-
national complications involved. Thus the JIJI on
August 11 gave a luke-warm welcome to the proposed
legislation, because of the stimulating effect the
manufacture of automobiles in Japan would have on other
Japanese industries, but warned the Government that it
should not interfere with the interests of foreign firms
in Japan and should not cause the Japanese industry to
come into rivalry with the foreign industry. There
should be cooperation rather than rivalry, and there-
fore the Government should proceed with caution. The
TOKYO ASAHI on August 13 welcomed the proposed measure
because it would strengthen the national defense and
develop

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
15
-8-
develop industry, but it regretted that the measure
would cause unavoidable friction with foreign auto-
mobile interests. The JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL on August 16
reviewed the circumstances giving rise to the proposed
legislation and expressed the opinion that the legis-
lation would not spell the doom of the foreign automobile
companies in Japan, because the American motor companies
have on their side technical skill, immense resources
and, above all, the tremendous American automobile market,
which permits of mass production and low prices. The
newspaper was also of the opinion that foreign motor-
car manufacturing concerns could look forward to many
years of profitable business in Japan, if they worked
with Japanese capital and control.
Treaty Rights.
The attention of the Department is respectfully
invited to the fact that the proposed legislation might
possibly be considered as an infringement of Article I
of the American-Japanese Treaty of Commerce and Navi-
gation of l9ll. Article I of the treaty provides that
Citizens or subjects of each of the High Contracting
Parties shall have liberty . . . generally to do any-
thing incident to or necessary for trade upon the same
terms as native citizens or subjects, submitting them-
selves to the laws and regulations there established".
The terms under which American firms will be able to
assemble and sell motor-cars in Japan, if and when the
proposed legislation becomes effective, will not be
exactly

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
/51
-9-
exactly the same as the terms under which the Japanese
firms will be able to produce and sell cars. The
American firms will be restricted in their operations,
unless they permit Japanese financial control of their
companies, while the Japanese firms will not be labor-
ing under any such restriction, being already Japanese.
The Embassy believes that somewhat similar cases have
arisen in California in the past, in connection with
Japanese owned or controlled corporations, and it
suggests a thorough study of this phase of the subject.
Respectfully yours,
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

Bureau of Industry Correspondence
August 9, 1935.
No. 7338, 1935.
Mr. R. A. May,
Representative of General Motors,
Japan, Limited.
Automobile manufacture on mass production basis.
The automobile industry is one of the fundamental
manufactures and occupies a position of great importance
not only in industry and transportation but also in nation-
al defense. In consideration of the importance of the in-
dustry the Japanese Government, which has made and is making
necessary arrangements for its control, has recently deter-
mined the fundamental policy to govern the manufacture of
ordinary vehicles on a mass production basis, as indicated
in the appended outline which is submitted for your perusal.
This office will be pleased to answer any inquiries
which you may have on this subject.
Shinsuke Kishi, Director of Industrial Affairs,
Department of Commerce and Industry.

=== Page 48 === (confidence 1.00)
153
Enclosure No. /
to despatch
No.1440 of Aug.22,1935from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
ESSENTIAL POINTS OF AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY LAW.
1. Enterprises for the manufacture of principal
parts and the construction of ordinary automobiles
shall be licensed enterprises but permission shall not
be necessary where output does not exceed a specified
limit. The principle of licensing, taking into account
the demand and the supply, shall be to grant permission
only to one or more companies and not to grant permission
to others.
2. The organizations which are qualified to receive
the aforementioned permission shall be limited to corpora-
tions of which a majority of the stock is owned by Japan-
ese subjects, or to corporations which, established as
juridical persons under Japanese laws and ordinances, are
controlled in a majority of their voting rights by Japan-
ese subjects.
3. Regarding the automobile manufacturing industry
which has received the permission referred to in the first
paragraph, such supervision and regulations shall be established
as may be necessary for industrial and national defense reasons.
4. As regards the already existing automobile in-
dustry corresponding to the definitions of the first para-
graph, its rights and interests shall be recognized within
the limits existing at the time of the determination of
this

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.96)
154
- 2 -
this policy, (i.e. August 9, 1935), and the accomplish-
ment of such enterprises shall be permitted; in regard
to new undertakings established thereafter, and extensions,
such rights and interests shall not be admitted when the
law is hereafter enforced.

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
155
Enclosure No.  to despatch
No.1440 of Aug. 22, 1935from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
MEMORANDUM
Conversation
August 20, 1935.
Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of
Commercial Affairs, Foreign Office.
Mr. Dickover.
Automobile Industry Legislation.
I called on Mr. Kurusu at 12 noon, by appointment,
and stated that I had studied the essential points of
the proposed automobile industry legislation, as an-
nounced by the Department of Commerce and Industry, but
that I could not understand the drift of the proposed
legislation from the summaries issued by that Department.
For one thing, I could not understand exactly what cars
were to be included in the legislation from the word
"taishusha" (great-people-cars) used in the official
summary of the legislation.
Mr. Kurusu said the word - a new one - meant "auto-
mobiles for the people", as an actual translation, but in
fact, in the proposed legislation it referred only to Ford
and Chevrolet, which were produced in Japan in large quanti-
ties. He said that if someone wanted to produce cars like
the Rolls-Royce or Packard in Japan in small quantities, the
law would not be applied to such a manufacturer.
I then asked if the word "kumitate" (set up or construct)
as used in the first paragraph of the summary referred to
assembly of parts as well as to complete construction of
cars.

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
156
- 2 -
rs.
Mr. Kurusu said that the word meant "assembly".
I then said that the fourth paragraph of the summary
did not seem to agree with the first and second paragraphs.
The first paragraph provided that anyone producing cars
for the great mass of the people had to have a license to
operate, while the second paragraph stipulated that such
a licensed manufacturer must be Japanese or controlled
by Japanese. The fourth paragraph, on the other hand,
stipulated that the rights and interests of existing auto-
mobile manufacturers would be recognized to the limit of
their present production. I asked if that meant that Ford
and General Motors could go on producing their present out-
put without license and without yielding control to Japanese
capital.
Mr. Kurusu said that that was the way he understood
the proposed legislation. Of course, he said, the actual
law had not yet been drafted, and he could not say exactly
what the provisions of the law would be, but he understood
that the purpose was to let Ford and General Motors continue
to operate on their present basis without giving control
to Japanese capital.
Mr. Kurusu then said that the purport of the proposed
legislation would be clear if one thing were understood;
namely, that the law was intended solely to prevent Ford
from oppressing the domestic manufacture of automobiles.
He said that the military wanted to build up a native
automobile industry in Japan, for national defense pur-
poses. Now, if Ford went ahead with his plan to build a
big

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
157
- 3 -
big factory in Japan and produce cars in still larger
quantities and at still cheaper prices, no Japanese auto-
mobile manufacturing company would have a chance to suc-
ceed for a long time, perhaps twenty or thirty years. It
would be like a child wrestling against a giant. So, said
Mr. Kurusu, the military hastily formulated this proposal
to pass legislation to control the motor-car industry in
order to restrain Ford and to prevent him building his new
large factory in Yokohama. But, Mr. Kurusu said, if Ford
should comply with the proposed law and take in sufficient
Japanese capital to give Japanese a controlling interest
in the local Ford company, the law would be useless and
would not fulfill its purpose. Ford could then expand as
much as he pleased and could continue to oppress the domes-
tic automobile industry. But, said Mr. Kurusu, the military
have been banking on the well-known aversion of Mr. Henry
Ford to letting the control of any of his companies pass out
of his hands. However, it would not be difficult to cir-
cumvent the law, Ford could give nominal control to some
Japanese in his employ, but actually retain the control.
I said that I thought that Ford would hardly stoop to
such practices, as he was known for his straight-forward
and direct methods.
I then said that the apparent intention of making
the proposed law retroactive to August 9, 1935 ( the date
of the announcement of the proposal) would work a hardship
on

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.97)
158
-4 -
In Ford, as the company had just bought a tract of land
in Yokohama for the purpose of extending the factory. Mr.
Kurusu said that he understood that the military had told
Asano, before he sold the land to Ford, that he (Asano)
must warn the local Ford people that restrictive laws were
going to be put into force. He said that Asano had done so,
but that Ford had insisted on going ahead with his project
anyway.

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)

DEPARTMENTOF STATE
RECEIVED
DFFARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
1935 MOV 18 PM 1 55
October 35, 1935.
ONISION OF
FE:
Mr. Dooman:
Confirming our discussions based on the suggestion
894.797/
of the Embassy at Tokyo that consideration be given to
invoking Article I of the Treaty of 19ll with Japan in
protest against the proposed automobile control law, I
am of the opinion that the article in question reason-
ably could be held to have been violated by the proposed
restriction on the production of motor vehicles by Ameri-
can companies in Japan if that proposal should be made ef-
feotive.
It is not improbable that the Japanese Government will
contend that the rights granted to Americans under the
article "to do anything incident to or necessary for trade
1935.
upon the same terms as native citizens or subjects",oul
问
be given its literal interpretation in the sense that,
ILd
030
since Japanese subjects are not permitted to operate auto-
mobile factories without restriction of output unless they
are financially and administratively controlled by Japanese,
the provisions of the proposed law simply require
American companies to operate on the same basis. It
would appear to be obvious, however, that this interpre-
F/G
tation

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
6
-2
tation is not tenable and could not have been intended
by either of the parties to the treaty. While the
article might have been more precisely phrased, it is be-
lieved that it should be interpreted in the sense that
Americans and Japanese were to be placed on a footing of
absolute equality in all matters pertaining to or incident
to trade, and that such equality would be clearly denied
by permitting Japanese subjects to manufacture and dis-
tribute motor vehicles without limitation as to quantity
while imposing such a limitation on American companies.
The operation of American automobile companies in Japan
appears now to be permitted on a basis of equality with
Japanese companies. Both Americans and Japanese are per-
mitted to operate - without restriction of output - under
their own management without condition as to the degree
of financial control by any nationality or as to the
nationality of any of the administrative supervision of
the companies. Should the proposed law become effective
there would be a material disorimination against American
companies as compared with Japanese, and it is obvious
that the treaty article in question was intended to
prevent just such discrimination.
Clear evidence of this fact is found in the original
proposal of the Japanese as to the provisions of Article I
of the treaty. That proposal contemplated that the
nationals

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.98)
11
-3
nationals of each of the contracting parties should re-
ceive in the territories of the other treatment with
respect to trade on a most-favored-nation basis and not
on a basis of national treatment. That proposal was re-
jected by this Government and the provisions of Artiole I
of the present treaty were substituted in its stead, pro-
viding thereby for national treatment instead of most-
favored-nation treatment. The mere mention of this fact
should be sufficient to establish the validity of the con-
tention that the proposed law would, if enacted, contravene
the treaty provision in question, but adequate support
for that contention is believed to be found in the practical
interpretation of the treaty article independently of any
comparison between the accepted draft and the rejected
Japanese draft.
While it would appear to be undesirable to make any
unnecessary reference to the interpretation of the treaty
article by the courts in the United States in view of the
fact that any consideration of those judicial decisions
involves reference to the disability of Japanese to own
lands in the States which have prohibited such ownership,
it might be feasible to refer inoidentally to the fact
that decisions of the courts in the United States have
definitely recognized the treaty right of Japanese subjects
to engage in trade in the United States on a basis of
fu11

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
full equality with American citizens. Such a decision
was made in the case of Tashiro et al vs. Jordan, decided
by the Supreme Court of California in l927 and reported
in 356 Pacific, page 545. In that opinion the court re-
ferred specifically to twat part of the treaty article in
question and said:
"But the right 'to carry on trade' in the
specifio lines of business mentioned in the treaty,
such as conducting manufactories, warehouses, and
shops and leasing lands for commercial purposes,
is from the very terms of said treaty as extensive
and complete as that employed by native citizens or
subjeots. It would be difficult to frame language
much more comprehensive than that found in the fol-
lowing provision of Article I of the treaty:
'and generally to do anything inoident to
or neoessary for trade upon the same terms
as native oitizens or subjects'.
This language follows the provision enumerating the
different classes of trade or business in which resi-
dent aliens are authorized to engage and must be read
in connection with such provision, and as so read it
gives to such aliens an equal standing with native
citizens or subjects in the conduct of those enumerated
classes of business which may be carried on by such
aliens."
The judicial opinion above quoted is an express recognition
that Article I of the Treaty of 19ll assures Japanese sub-
jeots full equality with citizens of the United States in
oarrying on trade in this country. It would appear to be
clear, therefore, that the Japanese Government is reciprocally
obligated to acoord to American citizens in Japan the same
privileges as are confirmed by the opinion mentioned to
Japanese

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.96)
-5
Japanese subjects in the United States. I am of the
opinion, therefore, that if there is any probability that
the proposed law will be made applicable to American
companies in Japan, it would be proper to instruct the
Embassy to inform the Foreign Office that in the opinion
of this Government the proposed law if made applicable
to American automobile companies in Japan would be a
clear violation of the rights to which those companies
are entitled by the treaty article under consideration.
894.797/11

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's
despatch No. 1440, dated August 22, 1935, in regard
to the proposed legislation designed to place the
automobile manufacturing and assembling industry of
Japan under a system of control by means of licenses,
in order to promote the development of a native
Japanese automobile industry.
F/FG.
The

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Embassy has been keeping in touch with the
two American manufacturers of motor-cars in Japan, the
Ford Motor Company of Japan, Limited, and General Motors
Japan, Limited, and has learned that, while General
Motors has not decided upon its future course of action
and is awaiting developments, the Ford Motor Company
intends to proceed with the construction of its new
factory in Yokohama, although it knows that the proposed
legislation is directed primarily at that Company. The
Company does not believe that the proposed legislation
will come into force (if it ever passes the Diet) for
NOT FOR PUBLICATION
at least one or two years, and wishes to present the
Japanese Government with a fait accompli in the shape
of a completed factory at that time, in the belief that
the Government could not refuse to permit the Company
to use a factory already completed. Even if the Govern
ment does not permit the factory to be used to its full
capacity, the Company will have a larger and more ef-
ficient assembling plant than it now possesses, for such
output as the Japanese Government will permit. The local
manager of the Ford Motor Company, moreover, does not
believe that the proposed automobile industry law will
be any more successful than the petroleum industry law
already in force. He thinks that the Army, which spon-
sored the recently announced automobile policy, will
soon find that it cannot obtain an adequate supply of
good cars and trucks from a native Japanese automobile
industry

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
industry; that the Japanese people will object to the
higher cost of cars which will inevitably follow
NOT FOR PO ICATION
manufacturers; that the automobile legislation is being
advocated by the more radical group in the Army, which
is now losing power; and that the Diet itself, with the
lessons of the unworkable petroleum control law before
it, is likely to balk at passing further legislation of
the kind.
Motor Truck Monopoly in "Manchukua".
Reference is made to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1067, dated November 28, 1934, on the subject of
the proposed automobile industry control in Japan and
Manchukuo". According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
September 3, 1935, which translates an article from the
NIKKAN KOGYO SHIMBUN, the Army is now apparently intend-
ing to follow up its advocacy of restrictive policies
on foreign automobile manufacturing companies in Japan
with a plan for preserving the Manchurian market for
Japanese automobile companies. The plan was decided
upon, according to the journal, by the Kwantung Army,
the South Manchuria Railway and the Ministry of Industry
of "Manchukuo", all of which have administrative powers
over the Dowa Automobile Company of Mukden (the automo-
bile producing company established in the former arsenal
at Mukden). The ADVERTISER translation of the article
follows:
nTWO

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
"Two policies have been fixed. The
first is that all truck chassis used in
Manchukuo must be provided by Dowa Automo-
bile unless the Kwantung Army staff gives
special permission. The second is that
only Japan-made cars may be used in private
motorbus enterprises. Regulations to ac-
complish these two aims are being drawn
and the South Manchuria Railway Company,
recently ordered about 50 Ford and Chevrolet
machines, is reported about to cancel its
contracts.
"Due to these two protective measures,
which are in line with the steps to protect
Japanese motor makers recently decided by
the Japanese Cabinet at the instance of the
War and Commerce and Industry Ministries,
Dowa is to expand. It will call in its
unpaid shares. The company is capitalized
at ¥8,000,000, of which the Manchukuo
Government subscribed ¥2,O00,00 and
¥6,000,000 was supplied by seven Japanese
automobile companies. Of the ¥6,000,000
only ¥1,500,000 has been paid up. The
company intends to call ¥1,500,000 at this
time. Of the money to be obtained, ¥300,000
will be applied to the deficit now on the
company's books and the remainder will go
toward erection of a factory in North China
and back to the subscribers in payment for
finished cars and parts."
Respectfullyyours,
Etim hatle
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

January 29, 1936.
Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1631,
January 9, 1936, amplifies its telegram
No. 6, January 9, 11 a.m., on the sub-
ject of Mr. Neville's conversation with
the Japanese Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs with regard to the proposed
logislation to place the automobile
manufacturing industry of Japan under a
system of license control.
On January 9 Mr. Neville saw the
Vice Minister and carried out the
Department's instruction contained in
the last paragraph of the Department's
No. 882, November 18, 1935, in which
the Embassy was authorized to discuss
orally and informally with the Foreign
Office the proposed legislation and to
inform the Foreign Office that in the
opinion of this Government the enact-
ment and enforcement of the proposed
law would be a clear violation of the
rights to which American nationals en-
gaged in the business of manufacturing
and distributing motor vehicles in
Japan are entitled by Article 1 of the
Treaty of 19ll. In the conversation
between Mr. Neville and the Vice Minis-
ter it was recognized on both sides
that

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
that the discussion was limited by the
fact that an exact draft of the pro-
posed legislation was not available,
but the conversation nevertheless took
fairly definite form. The Vice Minis-
ter was interested in knowing whether
Mr. Neville was speaking by direction
of the American Government, and Mr.
Neville acknowledged that he was. Dur-
ing the conversation the Vice Minister
consulted the Treaty of l9ll and re-
marked that the treaty does not provide
for rights of manufacture. He added
that offhand he did not see where the
treaty enters into the subject. Mr.
Neville replied that, according to
press accounts of the proposed legisla-
tion, the right of assembly and manu-
facture, which is incident to and neces-
sary for the trade in automobiles,
would apparently be infringed.
The Vice Minister indicated that
personally he is opposed to the prin-
ciple of the proposed law, and he showed
by his remarks that this is another of
those subjects in which the Foreign Of-
fice may be expected to give the impres-
sion of being unwillingly driven by
other branches of the Japanese Govern-
ment.
The despatch and enclosures should
be read in full.
COMMENT:

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
COMMENT :
In my opinion the Embassy has suc-
cessfully complied with the Department's
authorization of an oral and informal
discussion with the Foreign Office and
has accomplished a desirable result in
letting the Japanese Government know
that we are expecting to stand by the
Treaty of l9ll. In my opinion the sub-
jeot should be kept alive by further
conversations from time to time with of-
ficials in the Foreign Office. It is
probable that the recent dissolution of
the Japanese Diet means that enactment
of the proposed legislation is not immi-
nent at the present time, but the matter
will certainly be brought up later. In
the meantime a persistent reiteration of
our contention should be of advantage.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's tele-
gram of today's date in regard to the proposed legis-
2861 13
Aation for controlling the motor car industry in Japea
and to enclose a memorandum of the conversation which
the Counselor of the Embassy had with the Vice Minister
For Foreign Affairs on this subject. The Vice Minister
appeared somewhat surprised to learn that the American
Government felt that a treaty question was involved,
and was naturally unwilling off hand to admit that there
was
F/FG

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.98)
17
-2-
was a question, but stated that he would try to obtain
a draft of the proposed law and confer with the Embassy
later.
The conversation was entirely friendly and frank.
The Vice Minister gave the impression that he had been
left in the dark by other Departments of the Government,
and although he betreyed no irritation, he said that he
personally did not like this type of legislation. He had
no copy of the legislative draft, he said, and apparently
had not been consulted by other branches of the Govern-
ment in regard to it. He said that it was quite possible
that the law would not be enacted, as the Diet might be
dissolved before anything could be done, or repeat its
performance of last year when practically none of the
Government's legislative program was adopted. I may add
that the Vice Minister's avowed dislike of the proposed
law appears to be general in Foreign Office circles. It
remains to be seen, however, whether the Foreign Office
will be able to exert effective influence on the rest of
the Government.

Memorandum.
Conversation
Januery 8, 1936
Mr. Neville end Mr. Shigemitsu,
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Proposed Automobile Law.
I called on the Vice Minister shortly before noon.
(I was delayed nearly half an hour en route by the passage
of the Imperial party returning from the parade ground).
I told ir. Shigemitsu that I had come to discuss the pro-
posed automobile law, and invited his attention to the
announcement made to the automobile interests by the De-
partment of Commerce and Industry on August 9, 1935 (de-
spatch No. 1440, August 22, 1935, enclosure 1), and to
the news items appearing in the press on December 28,
(copy attached) last, which purported to give the gist
of a proposed new law to control the manufacture and as-
sembly of automobiles in Japan. I said that apparently
this law would provide for the licensing of automobile
construction in any form and restrict the business to
Japanese owned or controlled concerns, while foreign
factories and consequently trade in and distribution of
automobiles would be required to remain in the status
they occupied on August 9, 1935. I said that my Govern-
ment considered the proposed legislation out of line with
Article 1 of the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation of
191l because it denied equality of treatment with Japa-
nese subjects to American citizens engaged in the auto-
mobile trade in Japan, and that in the opinion of the
American

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.98)
1
- 2 -
American Government the enactment and enforcement of the
proposed law would be a clear violation of the rights to
which American nationals engaged in the business of manu-
facturing and distributing motor vehicles in Japan are
entitled by the treaty article under reference .
The Vice Minister asked me if I was speaking by direction
of the Government at Washington. I said that I was, but that
the Ambassador wished me to take up the matter orally and
informally with the Foreign Office and find out, if possible,
what form the projected legislation would take. Mr. Shi-
gemitsu then remarked that we were both speeking more or less
in the dark, as there appeared to be no copy of the pro-
posed law available. He said that he would try to get one.
He then consulted the treaty, and seid that it did not pro-
vide for rights of menufacture; that every Government re-
served certain industries to their own nationals and that
off hand he did not see where the treaty entered into it.
I said that I was not able to state exactly where the law
might violate the treaty, as we did not have the law before
us, but that the principle announced on August 9, and the
press accounts of the legislation that appeared on Decem-
ber 28 seemed to discriminate against Americans engaged
in the automobile trade in Japan; that, irrespective of
the specific right of manufacture, assembly and manu-
facture under present dey practise are incident to and
necessary for the trade in automobiles; that without
rights to assemble motor cars, there could be very little
trade in the commodity. To impose such conditions on
Americans would be clearly discriminatory in contravention
of the terms of the treaty. I added that, like him, I was
not

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
8
-3 -
not prepared to argue the matter in detail in the ab-
sence of more information as to the exact provisions of
the proposed law, but I wished to tell him of the Amer-
ican Government's attitude and to ask him whether the
question of treaty rights had been or would be fully
considered. He said that of course the treaty position
would have to be very carefully considered but that he
had not yet seen any draft of the law. He then added that
it was quite possible that no legislation of the kind
would be enacted, as the Diet might be dissolved, but
that he would go into the matter and communicate with me
later. He asked me if I should be willing to talk later
with one of the bureau chiefs. I seid that naturelly I
should.
In the course of the interview, which was friendly
throughout, Mr. Shigemitsu indicated that personelly be
did not like the principle of the proposed law, as what
he felt wes needed was more and cheaper eutomobiles; that
if let alone, Japanese would naturally acquire interests
in the foreign automobile plants, and the industry would
develop as the electrical and other industries had done.
I agreed with him and asked if he could help to avert the
present difficulty. He did not answer directly, but re-
iterated that he did not like this type of legislation.
E。 L。 N.

The draft of the automobile manu-
facture law was drawn up by the
Department of Commerce and In-
dustry at the Departmental confer-
ence held Friday at the official re-
sidence of the Minister of Commerce
and Industry. The draft will be
submitted to the present Diet ses-
don for approval.
Ever sinee last year the Govern-
ment has recognized the necessity
of controling the manufacture of au-
tomobiles, and has been studying the
question from all sides, particularly
with the view to fos'ering the pro-
gress of the industry in Japan.
Main Points Given
The main points in the drafted re-
gulation are as follows:
1. Those desiring to manufacture
automobiles in Japan must obtain
the Government's sanetion.
The
standard production capacity for se-
curing the Government sanetion is
6,000 cars a year.
2. Automobile manufacturing conm-
panies to be sanctioned under the law
should be companies in which a
majority of deciding votes are con-
trolled by Japanese subjects or con-
poration organized by Japanese sub-
jects under Japanese law.
3. Automobile manufacturing com-
panies organized under this law shall
be exempt from the business profit
tax. the income tax and other local
taxes during a specified period.
4. Automobiles companies sanction-
ed under this law may issue
debentures in amounts greater than
those designated by the Commerelal
Code.
. The import of certain automo-
bile parts or machinery may be ex-
empted from import tariff.
May Prevent Imports
6. The. Government may issue to
automobile companies formed under
this law orders based on national
defence nécessities.
7. Accórding to public necessity
the Government may order automo-
bile companies to alter prices or
equipment
8. For protecting the domestic
automobile industry; the Government
may take necessary steps to pre-
vent the import of foreign cars.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
RTMENT OF STATE
ON OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 3, 1936
Reference:
Tokyo's telegram No. 22,
February 1, noon.
The proposed automobile legislation
in Japan would presumably restrict the
operations of American interests in Japan
by giving substantial advantages in the
automobile industry to Japanese corpora-
tions from the ownership of which aliens
would be excluded. Production of auto-
mobiles in Japan by American interests at
the present time is by companies incorporat-
ed under Japanese law. The Japanese Govern-
ment will apparently take the position that
the exclusion of American c itizens from the
right to own controlling shares in Japanese
corporations which will enjoy full
privileges under the proposed legislation
will not violate the Treaty of 19ll.
Article I of the Treaty of l9ll gives to
American citizens the right generally to
do anything incident to or necessary for
trade upon the same terms as native
subjects of Japan. The s ame article also
assures to American citizens the same
rights and privileges as may be granted to
native subjects in the protection of
property, on submission to the conditions
imposed upon native subjects.
Article VII has to do with the rights
in Japan of companies incorporated in the
United States and is not pertinent to the
rights of General Motors Japan and the Ford
Company of Japan.

May 25, 1936.
Noto:
I took up the matter of the Japanese
automobile industry law today with
Mr. Metzger.
Mr. Metsger is familiar with the
matter, as he took part in a conference
in Mr. Hackworth's office, when the ques-
tion was discussed of a possible basis for
representations to the Japanese Government.
Mr. Metzger stated to me that, in the view
of Le, American industrial corporations
have no standing whatever in Japan except
such standing as may be in conformity with
Japanese law, but that American individuals,
whether residing in the United States or
in Japan, have the right under treaty to
incorporate in Japan or do anything else
"incident to or necessary for trade".
I said to Mr. Metzger that in my
opinion the attached telegram drafted by
Mr. Ward did not square with the facts,
which are, in my understanding, that
majority interests in General Motors of
Japan and Ford Motors (Japan) are held
respectively, and notby the Messrs.
du Pont, Sloane and Raskob in the one
case and by Henry Ford in the other.
It is the opinion of Mr. Metzger that
the first thing we should do is to ascertain
the

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.90)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
the facts in regard to the organization and
owmership of the Japanese corporations
above-named. Mr. Metzger then stated that
if the facts are as I understand them to
be the telegram drafted by Mr. Ward should
not go. If, however, it were ascertained
that the Japanese corporations have
American individuals as majority-owners,
he thought that the telegram is satisfactory
and should be sent.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 28, 1936.
Mr. Hornbeck:
Subject: Proposed Japanese
Automobile Legislation.
Herewith a draft of a telegram to Tokyo in
regard to proposed Japanese legislation
governing the manufacture of automobiles in
Japan. The draft has been prepared by Mr. Ward
of Le.
If the telegram is to go, I think it
should be addressed directly to Tokyo (rather
than to Shanghai) and be sent in confidential
oode.
You will recall that there are involved
in this case complicated questions of legal
interpretation. Memoranda discussing the
proposed legislation and the legal principles
involved, prepared by Mr. Coville, Mr. Ward
and Mr. Hackworth, appear immediately under
the draft telegram.
Both Mr. Dooman and I are not entirely
convinced that we are on indisputably clear
legal ground in the attached draft telegram.
If the basis of argument in the attached
draft telegram is sound, would it be consistent
to deny to Japanese (or Belgian) individuals
the right to form Philippine corporations to
engage in public works construction in the
Philippine

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Philippine Islands? You will recall that a
Philippine law does deny this right to alien
individuals. It may be argued that public
works represent a special category outside the
general rights granted by treaties. But if this
Government should use this argument, could not
the argument be turned against us to our
disadvantage by Japan or by countries where
state control of industries is more or less
universal (for example, Soviet Russia or
Italy).
The basis of argument used in the attached
draft telegram might be tenable in the case of
American individuals residing in Japan. If,
however, the Japanese should admit this but
contend that American corporations in the
United States (as distinct from American
Individuals residing in Japan) have no right
to set up corporate entities in Japan, I am
not sure that we would have any sound legal
basis to answer such an argument.
We have had reports to the effect that
the special session of the Japanese Diet which
will be convened in May will give consideration
to the question of enacting automobile control
legislation. We, however, have not been
requested by the American interests concerned
to take any action on their behalf.
I think that the attached draft telegram
represents the best line of argument that the
Department

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 28, 1936.
I have brought this matter
to Mr. Phillips' attention,
recommending that we hold the
proposed instruction in reserve;
and he approves so doing.

Department can put forward. But it is, as I
have indicated, open to question.
After you examine the file, I would like
to discuss the case with you.

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 17, 1936.
RCM:
SKH:
The attached draft telegram in regard
to the proposed Japanese automobile law
was prepared by Mr. Ward.
The argument that the rights conferred
by a treaty specifically upon individual
citizens automatically accrue to corpora-
tions, is not on all fours with the con-
tention of Le, as expressed in the case
of the complaint of the Belgian Ambassador
in regard to the terms of a Philippine law
covering contracts for Philippine public
works.
Mr. Ward in explaining this difference
stated that public funds used to defray the
cost of public works are the common property
of a people, and that therefore the people
may lay down any restrictions which seem
to them to be wise in regard to the dis-
posal of such funds; that this doctrine
had been upheld on several occasions by
the courts of the United States. He
therefore saw no conflict between the
position taken by Le in the Belgian case
and the position which it takes in the
Japanese automobile law case.
I am not entirely satisfied with the
argument put forward in Mr. Ward's draft,
but

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2 -
but as this is a legal case I have
accepted it.
There is also attached to the file a
draft telegram prepared by Mr. Coville,
which Mr. Ward did not find acceptable.
Note: I understand from Mr. Vallance
that the Belgian case is being studied by
Mr. Hackworth, who has been having
Mr. Vallance prepare certain materials
for examination in connection therewith.
Mr. Vallance is arranging to have sent out
the draft note to the War Department,
transmitting a copy in translation of the
Japanese law in regard to Jananese public
works.

Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1650
transmits memorandum of a private con-
versation at a social engagement on
January 2l between Mr. Neville and the
Japanese Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
(Mr. Shigemitsu) in which the Vice Minister
asked Mr. Neville if Mr. Neville still
wished to discuss the proposed legislation
to control the automobile industry. When
Mr. Neville indicated that he would like
to do so the Vice Minister replied that
such a conversation would be of no use
because the law had not been completely
drafted and because there is little
prospect of enactment of that particular
piece of legislation, in view of the
dissolution of the Diet and the expecta-
tion that the spring session of the new
Diet will be necessarily short.
COMMENT:
See Tokyo's telegram No. 22,
February 1, 1936, noon, and memorandum
of February 10, 1936, prepared by Mr. Ward
in Le.
Mr. Shigemitsu is possibly correct
in anticipating that the proposed auto-
mobile legislation will not be enacted
within several months. In my opinion
the

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
present is nevertheless the best
 to make clear to the Japanese Foreign
rfice that the American Government finds
in the Japanese Government's interpreta-
tion of articles I and VII of the Treaty
of 19ll, as stated by Mr. Shigemitsu,
nothing to alter the view of the American
Government that the Treaty of 19ll would
be violated by the enactment and enforce-
ment of the proposed automobile legislation.
The considerations which prompted the
Department to send instruction No. 882,
November 18, 1935, and telegraphic instruc-
tion No. 202, December 24, 1935, 1 p.m.,
have not substantially changed. Sub-
sequently the Embassy has reported (Tokyo's
telegram No. 22, February 1, 1936, noon)
a statement by Mr. Shigemitsu based on
an untenable interpretation of the Treaty
of l9ll. Refutation is desirable.
If we take exception to Shigemitsu's
arguments and it happens that the proposed
automobile legislation is not enacted,
we shall have suffered no loss.
If we take exception to Shigemitsu's
arguments and it happens that the proposed
automobile legislation is enacted, our
position will be the stronger whenever
retaliatory measures may be considered.
Tokyo's telegram is detailed and
clear, and I see no reason for awaiting
an amplifying despatch before issuing
an instruction.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegram

ED
1/
a memorandum of a conversation between the Counselor of
the Embassy and the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs.
The latter stated that the Japanese Government considered
that Article 1 of the treaty of l9ll applies to individuals,
and that it is unlikely that any individuals as such will
be permitted to manufacture automobiles. Thus there would
be no discrimination so far as individuals are concerned,
F/FG
whilo
LATY DIVISION
JUL 26 1937

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
while so far as companies or corporations are concerned,
there is a special article in the treaty (Article VII)
which regulates their position and sets forth their rights.
The Vice Minister also said that while the Foreign
Office does not like the bill, there are forces at work
beyond its control, and that there is a movement on foot
to have the bill put before the special session of the
Diet in the spring. He did not know whether this could be
done, he said, but the Government is under a good deal of
pressure to have the measure enacted.
From all the information which the Embassy is able
to gather, the Vice Minister appears to have judged the
situation correctly. The "forces beyond the control" of
the Foreign Office are determined to build up a purely
Japanese motor car industry. These forces are, so far as
the Embassy has been able to discover, partly military
and partly industrial. The latter wish complete protection
if they are to embark on motor car construction, and the
former are willing to use their influence in government
circles to that end in order to have a motor car industry
independent of outside influences . The Embassy is in-
clined to the view that legislation along the lines in-
dicated in previous despatches is bound to be enacted in
one form or another without much reference to the attitude
of outside interests. The Embassy has, from time to time,
reported on the rising tide of nationalism in Japan, and
the

- 3 -
the proposed legislation is merely another manifestation
of the same spirit.
