Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1659 of February 5, 1936
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Memorandum
Conversation
January 27, 1936
Mr. Neville and Mr. Shigemitsu,
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Automobile Control Law.
I saw Mr. Shigemitsu at noon. I told him that I had
come to ask him about the proposed automobile control law,
an explanation in regard to which he had promised me when
I last saw him. He said that he had looked into the matter
and had discovered that the papers had reported fairly
accurately what the Department of Commerce and Industry
had had in mind to put before the Diet. He said that there
was a movement on foot to have the bill put before the special
session of the Diet in the Spring, but he was not sure
whether that could be done. He repeated again what he had
told me before, that the Foreign Office does not like the
bill, but he added that there are forces at work beyond
their control. He said that the project appears to be a
popular one and that the Government is under a good deal of
pressure of one kind or another to get it enacted into law.
He said that the political situation is uncertain, that our
discussion must necessarily be academic because there is
no actual draft of the law at hand, and that finally, it
is impossible to say what form the legislation may finally
take.
He then came to the question of treaty right, stating
that it had been carefully considered. He said: "Article
1 of the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation of l9ll applies
to individuals as similar article of Treaties of Commerce
and

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
and Navigation concluded between Japan and other countries.
It seems likely that no individuals as such would be allowed
to go into the automobile manufacturing business under the
present automobile law. In that respect there is no dis-
crimination between Japanese subjects and foreigners resident
in Japan. So far as companies or corporations aro concerned,
there is a special article(Article VII) in the Treaty which
regulates their position and sets forth their rights. This
Article specifies that it has no bearing upon the question
whether a company or association organized in one of the two
countries will or will not be permitted to transact its
business or industry in the other, this permission remaining
always subject to the laws and regulations enacted or estab-
lished in the respective countries. It seems, therefore,
clear that the proposed automobile law, by which permission
to go into automobile industry will be reserved to national
companies or corporations, will not be inconsistent with
the stipulations of the Treaty, and that it is of exclu-
sive jurisdiction of the respective countries, as a matter
of administrative convenience, to determine in what condi-
tions national companies or corporations may be permitted
to go into such industry. In this respect, it should be
observed that two automobile companies under American
capitel(viz. Ford and General-Motors) were established and
registered as Japanese companies and, as such, they have been
transacting their business." I asked him if that could be
taken as the Japanese Government's position on the treaty.
He said that it could, so far as informal conversation can be
so considered, and remarked that while he, and the Foreign
office

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.93)
- 3 
Office generally, considered the project unwise, he would
not be in a position to state to other Departments that
there is a treaty obligation which would stand in the way
of the proposed legislation.
E。 L。 N.

pondence on the subject of the proposed legislation for con
trolling the automobile industry in Japan and its pro-
bable effect upon the American automotive interests in
Japan.
It is now reported that the Government intends to
introduce into the present special session of the Diet
旦
TREATY DIVISION
F/FG
DEP'RTMENT OF STATE

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
a bill containing the essential features of the auto-
mobile policy announced on August 9, l935, and described
in the Embassy's despatch No. 1440, dated August 22, 1935.
On May 2, 1936, some of the Japanese newspapers published
articles describing the proposed leislation which, from
their similarity, can be assumed to have been compiled
from a prepared statement issued presunebly by the De-
partment of Commerce and Industry. The gist of these
articles is as follows:
The principal reasons for the proposed le isletion
are, first, to build up a dumestic motor-cer industry
to the point were the local industry can sunply the local
merket for the less expensive makes of automobiles, for
this purpose taking advantage of the present moment
when the low yen exchange rate will give additional assist-
ance to the domestic industry; and, second, to insure
national defense by having available an industry capable
of supplying the needs of the Army for motor transport
and motorized units in time of emergency. Because
of the paramount importance of these reasons, the Govern-
ment has decided to seek enactment of the law for the con-
trol of the automobile industry, "in spite of the delicate
and complex problems of international relations which are
involved."
Although the number of motor-cars in Japan has increased
more than eight-fold in twelve years (to 135,000 in October,
1935), there are still only 16 cars per ten thousand of the
inhabitants

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
inhabitants of the Empire, as compared with 1,895 cars
per ten thousand inhabitants in the United States. 96
per cent of the automobiles in use in Japan are the products
of the Ford Motor Company and the General Motors Compeny.
The manufacture of automobile parts and accessories
in Japan, however, has developed rapidly in recent years,
indicating that Japanese manufacturers are capable of
automobile manufacture, and therefore encouraging the
Gover: ment to propose the law. In 1918 motor-car manu-
facture in Japan was encouraged by meens of military
subsidies to producers of trucks; in 193l a comnittee
was esteblished to study the firm establishment of an auto-
mobile industry in Janan; and in 1934 an inter-departmental
conference was established, composed of officials of the
Ministries of iar, Navy, Railways, Home Af airs, Com-
merce and Industry, Foreign Affairs, Finance and Over-
seas Afrairs and of the Resources Bureau of the Cabinet,
to discuss measures for the establis ient of a nationel
policy for the automobile industry. This inter-depert-
mental conference compiled the basic plan, announced
on August 9 last, for the control of the industry, and
has now worked out the draft of the Automobile Industry
Law, which was approved by the Cabinet on April 28, 1936.
The essential features of this draft law are as follows
(according to the JAPAN TILES AND LAIL):
"1.

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4-
"1. Purpose of the Law
The purpose of the law is firmly
to establish the motor car manu-
facturing industry in Japan with
a view to attain completion of
the national defense and to ac-
hieve industrial development.
2. License System
(a) A license must be held by
everyone who assembles motor cars
which are designated by order of
the Government and everyone who
manufactures automobile parts which
ere designated by the Government,
in excess of a certain limit to
be fixed by the Goverment.
(b) Everyone who receives such
a license from the Government
must be a joint stock company
formed under the laws of Japan,
end half or more of the total
stock shares and voting privileges
in the said comnany must be in the
possession of Japanese subjects
or of companies organized under the
laws of Janan.
3. Privileges.
(a) Companies to which licenses
are granted shall be exempted from
certain classes of taxes ror a cer-
tain period.
(b) Special provisions will be
made in the Conmercial Codes in
order that fecilities may be
offered to automobile menufac-
turing companies in regard to in-
creases of canital and the float-
ing of debentures.
4. Protection of .ublic interests
(a) The Government is authorized
to issue to the automobile manu-
facturing companies, whenever
necessary, such orders as are deemed
necessary for supervision of the
industry and for promotion of the
public welfare.
(b) The Government is autnorized
to issue such orders as are aeemed
necessary for the national defense.
5. Regulations Governing Importation
of Automobiles and Parts.
(a)

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
(a) When the prices of impcrted automobiles
and imported automobile parts are considered
sufficiently low to have a harmful effect
upon the growth of the automobile manufacturing
industry in this country, the Government may
restrict the importation of, and impose
higher import duties on, the said automobiles
and parts thereof.
6. Automobile lianufacturing Industry
Commission.
An Automobile Manufacturing Industry Commission
shall be organized to consider matters re-
lating to licenses for automobile manufacturing
companies end to the enforcement of this law.
7. Retroactive Provision.
Those persons who begen the automobile manu-
facturing business, as defined in this law,
prior to August 9, 1935, and who are still
engaged in the same business at the time of
enforcement of this law, will be per.itted to
continue such business without holding the
aforementioned license but vithin the limit of
their business as it existed prior to August 9,
1935.
The newspapers express confidence that the proposed law
will be enacted during the present Diet session.
It will be observed that, accordin to the above
digest of the articles a pearin in the Japanese press,
the proposed law does not differ in any essential re-
spect from the policy announced on August 9, 1935. In
essence, the purpose of the law is to permit the two
American automobile assembling plants in Japan to con-
tinue operations up to the limit of their production on
August 9, 1935, but to secure to Jananese manufacturers
all the natural increase in the Japanese demand. The
American companies can continue to operate without ob-
taining licenses and without conceding control to Tapa-
nese interests but they will not be able to enlarge
their activities in Japan.
Attitude.

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Attitude of Ford and General Motors.
According to statements made to a momber of the
staff of the Embassy by the general ...anager for Japan
of the ord Motor Company, the Company is proceeding
with the erection of its new factory at Tsurumi, near
Yokohama, although the Company is avere that the pri-
mary purpose of the automobile policy as announced last
August was to prevent the Ford Motor Company from en-
larging its facilities in Japen.
. The Company is doing
this in the belief that the Ja anese will not be able
to produce good automobiles in sufricient quantities
to meet the increasing demand, and t at the Govern-
ment will have to fall back upon the American companies
for more cars to supply the deficit. In any case, the
new fecilities will permit cars to be produced more cheaply,
and even if the Company is limited to its present output,
increased profits will be possible.
As was announced last winter, eneral Motors Japan,
Limited, was negotiating with the ippon Sangyo Kabu-
shiki Kaisha for : merger of the two companies in order
to comply vith the provision of the proposed law that
all licensed automobile manufecturers be under Japanese
control. To facilitate the negotiations. officials of
the Nippon Sangyo interests proceeded to New Tork to
confer with officials of the General Motors Corporation.
It is now definitely reported, owever, that the nego-
tiations have reached a deadlock, partly on the question
of the degree of participation by the ippon Sangyo interests
in

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
in General Motors Japan, Limited, and partly on the
question of the retention, should the merger be con-
cluded, of the foreign employees of General Motors
Japan, Limited. It is elso renorted that the offi-
cers of the ivippon Sangyo interests are returning to
Japan, indicating tnat negotiations have been dis-
continued. Local officials of General Motors Japan,
Limited, however, profess to know nothing of the pro-
gress or lack of progress of the negotiations.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph . Grew.

The Embassy at Tokyo reports in its
despatch No. 1872 of May 29, 1936, that
the Automobile Industry Control Law was
passed by the Diet, but that as the law
has not been promulgated as yet no
authentic text is as yet available.
The Embassy understands, however, that
the bill as passed substantially conforms
to the terms of the policy announced on
August 9, 1935, with, however, one
important exception-that the duty on
automobiles or parts thereof may be
increased if it should appear that they
are being imported at prices so low as
to injure the domestic automobile industry.
It is the opinion of the Embassy that the
authority granted to employ this new and
additional measure reflects the determina-
tion of the military authorities to
proceed as rapidly as possible with the
development of a national automobile
industry.
Notwithstanding the passage of the
bill, the despatch makes no reference to
any request for assistance having been
made to the Embassy by the American
interests concerned.
No action required.

SUBJECT: AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY CONTROL LAW

The Secretary of State,
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

Sir:
24
JUL S 1936
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 1820
FILED
of May 12, 1936 and in amplification of the Embassy's
23
telegram No. 112, May 23, 7 p.m., I have the honor to
report that the Bill for the Control of the Automobile
Industry passed the lower house of the Diet on May 19
and the House of Peers on May 23. There was no op-
position on the floor of either house. As the law has
not

=== Page 131 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
not yet been promulgated and published in the Official
Gazette the authentic text is not yet available. A
translation thereof will be forwarded to the Department
at the earliest opportunity.
It is believed that the terms of the Bill are sub-
stantially those of the draft reported in the Embassy's
last despatch on this subject. These in turn conform
to the automobile policy announced on August 9, 1935
with the addition of one important provision. This
provision (No. 5 in the draft law) empowers the Govern-
ment to restrict the importation of, and impose higher
duties on, automobiles and automobile parts when the
prices thereof are considered to be so low as to affect
adversely the domestic automobile industry. Whereas
the policy originally announced would restrict the
American companies to the volume of business enjoyed
as of August 9, 1935, the addition of this provision em-
powers the Government to restrict their existing business
at will whenever it is considered that the price of im-
ported products is lower than Japenese manufacturers can
successfully meet. With the competitive superiority of
the American products generally admitted, the addition is
a potential threat to the American automobile interests
in Japan.
Upon considering the situation of the industry
here it seems likely that this drastic change in the
original policy was influenced by the limitations of the
present

=== Page 132 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
present Japanese market for motor vehicles. This market
amounts to roughly 30,000 vehicles annually of which the
American assembly plants can and do provide 25,000. Of
the balance of 50oo several thousand are normally taken
up by Chrysler products and the importation of higher
priced cars. There thus remains to Japanese producers
little more than the natural increase in the market.
It was, apparently, first thought that the natural
increase would be sufficiently rapid to provide a basis
for Japanese industry during the next few years. The
policy of August 9, 1935 ensuring the enjoyment of their
existing business to the American companies seems to have
been predicated on this view. Now, however, it appears
that the authorities are anxious to establish the domestic
industry at the earliest possible moment. The Government
consequently has obtained larger powers than was originally
contemplated, to be used, apparently in case the natural
increase should not be sufficient.
In an interpellation
in the House of Peers by Mr. Kenkichi Yoshizawa, the
Government was questioned as to whether it had any in-
tention of increasing the market for automobiles by
reducing the prohibitive luxury taxes upon them and by
improving roads. The importance and efficacy of measures
in this direction seem not yet to be recognized. There
is instead the evident determination to allocate the exist-
ing market by means of the new bill if it should seem
desirable.
The

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The fact is that with the American companies in
the field a market of only several thousand cars a year
remains to Japanese manufacturers and this is inadequate
to support an efficient and economic domestic industry.
In the opinion of the acting Commercial Attaché of the
Embassy the privileges and immunities contemplated by
the policy announced last summer would not have proved
sufficient to dislodge the American competitors. More
drastic measures were needed if Japan were determined
immediately to develop an automobile industry of her
own. In default of increased demand the American market
here would have to be largely taken over and the import
duties and restrictions authorized by the new law are
well calculated to accomplish this if necessary.
It must be remembered, however, that the law itself
merely provides the legal framework for the Government's
policy. Upon the regulations for its enforcement depends
the practical effect of the law; the regulations can
determine the fate of the American interests here.
Drastic regulations, operating through the import provi-
sions could eliminate the American interests. On the
other hand there are practical considerations which render
uncertain the adoption of extreme measures. Japan is ac-
customed to cheap motor transportation. Upwards of 90%
of the motor vehicles in operation are in commercial
use as trucks, buses, and taxis. To force this industry
to purchase Japanese made vehicles would seriously
increase

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
increase costs not only within the industry but to the
users of transportation, would deny to Japanese the
advantages of the annual technical refinements incor-
porated in the American makes, and would result in less
efficient operation on the roads. Some days ago in the
Diet the director of the engineering bureau of the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry stated that the results
of the use of Japan-made motor vehicles in Manchuria up
to a recent date have been comparatively unfavorable
(although the manufacturers thereof enjoy a liberal army
subsidy). It is believed that the small Datsun car,
which is by no means inexpensive, is selling below the
cost of production; it is anticipated that the new Toyoda
car will also sell at a loss. Japanese manufacturers
have so far been unable to secure the volume necessary
to cheap and efficient production; whether the enjoyment
of the entire Japanese market would enable them to do so
is a matter of serious doubt.
The practical disadvantages of carrying through the
automobile policy hastily are well known to the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry. Mr. Yoshino, the Vice-Minister,
recently intimated to the Counselor of the Embassy that
so long as he was in office the American interests need
not fear drastic regulations. Nevertheless, whether
Mr. Yoshino and those who feel as he does, can withstand
the pressure of the military which has in so many in-
stances crudely carried through its policies regardless
of

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
of costs, remains to be seen. The nature of the enforce-
ment regulations will be the first clue as to the result
of the conflict between the two opposing viewpoints. Ac-
cording to Mr. Yoshino it is not expected that the
regulations will be made public before the middle of
June.
The Embassy is informed that the Consulate at
Yokohama is preparing a report on the plans of Japanese
automobile manufacturers for expansion and for the in-
troduction of new models.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
25
No. 1872/of May 29, 1936, and to previous correspondence
aoncerning the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law and
1/
to enclose herewith a translation, prepared in the Embassy
of the terms of the law as published in the OFFICIAL GAZEITE
of May 29, 1936.
Although the provisions of the new law are in more de-
tail than was anticipated, there appears to be no difference
of principle between the actual text and the supposed draft
bill which was reported to the Department in the Embassy's
despatch No. 1820 of May 12, 1936. As was foreshadowed
in the draft bill, the Government has secured comprehensive
powers
FG

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
powers to control and direct the industry in its discretion.
It may be noted, moreover, that the law specifically
provides for inspection by Government officials not only
of the offices and business records of all automobile com-
panies, including the companies which are exempted from
the license provisions of the law, but also the factories,
warehouses and other sites owned by the companies. There
are other specific provisions empowering the Government to
fix prices in the case of any automobile manufacturing com-
pany and to direct companies to manufacture automobiles or
automobile parts for military use.
As mentioned in previous despatches the practical ef-
fect of the law depends on the enforcement regulations and
the Imperial Ordinance relating thereto has not yet been
promulgated. hen it appears the Embassy will report further
and will endeavor to comment thereon.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

AUTOMCLILE MAUFAGTURILG L.D STRY LAW.
(Law 1.0. 33, sanctioned May 23, 1936, and appearing
in the OFF1IL GATTE, My 29, 1936.)
Article 1. The object of this law is to strengthen
national defense und pronote industrial progress by es-
tablishing the autonobile man facturing industry in Japan.
Article 2. Automobile manufacturing industry, as the
term is used in this law means industries designated by
ordinance, wich engage in the asseniblying or manufacture
of automobiles or automobile parts.
Article 3. Persons who wish to enguge in the manufacture
of automobiles shall ottain a license from the Government,
unless the volume of utonobiles or automobile parts to be
manufactured or assemoled is less than a figure to be fixed
by ordinance.
The Governmont shall take into consideration the con-
dition of supply and demand for autonobiles and automobile
parts and unless it dee.is that no obstacle to the establish-
ment of the auto...obile manufacturing industry will result,
the Governient shall not crant the license mentioned in the
preceding paragraph.
Article 4. rersons ualificd to reccive licenses men-
tioned in the preceding article, shall be only joint stock
companies orcunized under Japanese law of which more than
one-half of the shareholders, more than one-half of the dir-
ectors, more than one-half the cupital and more than one-
half of the voting rights are or are held by Japanese sub-
jects or juridical persons organized under Jupanese law.
The juridical persons mentioned in the preceding para-
graph must be juridical persons of which more than one-half
of the members, shareholders or officers in charge of con-
ducting business and more than one-half of the amount of
capital or more than one-half of the voting rights are not
or do not belong to foreigners or foreign juridical persons.
If a person who has obtained the license mentioned in
the preceding article has ceased to possess the qualifica-
tions specified in the preceding two paragraphs, the license
granted shall be invalidated.
Article s. An automobile manufacturing company which has
obtained a license, mentioned in article 3, shall commence
operati ns within a period prescribed by the Governnent.
The Government may grant an extension of the period men-
tioned in the preceding paragraph only in case it recognizes
that there is good reason for so doing.
When an automobile manufacturing company fails to commence
operations within the period mentioned in the two preceding
paragraphs, the license prescribed in Article 3 shall be
invalidated.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 15, 1936.
RbM:
Tokyo's 1944 of July 14, 1936, sends
forward translations of various ordinances
and regulations for the enforcement of the
Automobile Industry Law.
I think it is necessary merely that
you note that the Law (including those
provisions which require that more than
50 percent of the capital stock shall be
owned by Japanese) is applicable only to
companies producing more than three thousand
units a year. The Embassy thinks that that
figure is somewhere close to the maximum
which any new Japanese manufacturer is
likely to produce for the next few years.
It is reported also that thought is
being given to increasing the duty on
automobiles and automobile parts to 50
percent ad valorem (it is now, I believe,
35 percent). Some people believe that
even this increase will not effectively
protect Japanese companies and that it may
be necessary to impose quotas, which would
adversely affect the American companies.
The Embassy remarks in conclusion that
the Japanese do not seem to be disposed to
act hastily, and it believes that the Law
will be administered for the present at
least in such manner as not to be injurious
to American interests.

Subject: Ford and General Motors of Japan.
With reference to your memorandum of June 22, we are informed
by Mr. R. I. Roberge, General Sales Department, Ford Motor Company,
that the capital stock of the Ford Motor Company of Japan Ltd.,
consists of 160,000 shares, all of which is owned by the Ford Motor
Company of Dearborn, with the exception of 6 shares held by American
citizens.
In turn, Ford Motor Company would like to know whether you
have received any information from Japan concerning the regulations
for enforcement of the Automobile-Monopoly Law recently passed by
the Japanese Diet.
R. L. Whaley,
Commercial Agent in Charge

The Honorable

Sir:
814.777/27
With reference to the mbassy's telegram No. 152
26
July 10, 5 p.m., and to the Embassy's despatch No. 1891
of June 11, 1936, I have the honor to report that ordi-
nances relative to the Automobile Manufacturing Industry
Law were published in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE of July 10, 1936,
STAE
and that the law went into effect on the following day. The
oginances were three in number, an Imperial Ordinance fix-
i the date of enforcement of the law, a second Imperial
AFA
Orminence regarding its enforcement, and an ordinance of the
Doartment of Commerce and Industry containing further de-
F/FG
fintions and regulations for enforcement. The texts of the
ordinances

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
ordinances, as translated by the Embassy, are forwarded
with this despatch.
As mentioned in the Embassy's telegram referred to
in the preceding paragraph the law does not apply to auto-
mobiles having engines of less than 750 cubic centimeters
cylinder displacement. Such automobiles,including the
Datsun, already receive certain privileges under a separate
law encouraging the production of small cars.
Also referred to in the Embassy's telegram was the
stipulation that the manufacturers to whom the law applies
are those producing more than 3,0oo cars per annum. The
press had reported that the Government had intended to fix
this figure at 5,ooo but that, inasmuch as the foreign auto-
mobile interests had been planning to circumvent the law by
establishing subsidiary companies, each of a capacity of less
than 5,Ooo vehicles annually and hence not subject to regula-
tion, the figure was reduced to 3,ooo.
However, a simpler explanation exists. The law is
designed to assist qualified Japanese manufacturers and,
in the present embryonic state of the domestic industry, five
thousand cars a year is a total quite beyond the capacity of
any Japanese manufacturer. However, two companies which
have lready commenced operations may within the near future
be expected to develop a capacity of three thousand cars per
year. These companies are the Toyoda Automatic Loom Company
and the Nissan Automobile Company. With regard to these
companies the ASAHI and the NICHI NICHI both claimed in
recent

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
recent articles that they had been selected by the Govern-
ment as the basis for the domestic industry, that they had
already filed formal reports of their plans with the Govern-
ment, and that the Government was planning to discourage
reported automobile manufacturing projects contemplated by
the Mitsui and Sumitomo interests on the grounds that the
market will be fully taken care of without them.
The foregoing report suggests a second reason for es-
tablishing the figure at 3,O00. The figure selected not
only determines those manufacturers to be favored but it
also determines those who are to come under the detailed
Government regulation prescribed by the law. By placing
the figure at a lower level the Government has taken a further
step to enable itself to have at all times complete informa-
tion with regard to the state of this industry. Under the
regulations prescribed by ordinance the Government now re-
quires comprehensive reports from qualified companies and
from those established before August 9, 1935 ( the foreign
companies).
The present ordinances are largely devoted to the proce-
dure for exemption from duty on imported parts and materials
for the industry and for the remission of income and business
profits taxes. These are the privileges which, it is hoped,
will enable Japan to establish an automobile industry. How-
ever, the new law provides two further and more drastic means
of encouraging Japanese producers at the expense of the
foreign companies. Import duties may be raised and import
quotas

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
quotas may be established. These provisions have not been
invoked in the present enforcement regulations but it is
believed on good authority that the Government is consider-
ing an increase in the duty upon automobiles and automobile
parts to 50% ad valorem. Most observers do not believe
that it is possible to effect the purpose of the law without
taking this step. Some further apprehend that even this
will not prove sufficient and that in the end it will be
necessary to est blish quotas whic of course would directly
curtail the business of the foreign co.panies.
However, as mentioned above no drastic action has been
taken in the present ordinances. There is manifest in them
no disposition to act hastily and to attempt to create a
Japanese industr over night. Therefore, although an increase
in the duties is probable, ror the present the enforcement
regulations appear to be liberal and not imediately injurious
to American interusts.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Imperial Ordinance No. 170.
Enforoing Ordinance for the Automobile
Manufacturing Industry Law.
Artiole l. The term automobiles as used in
Article 2 of the Automobile Manufacturing Industry
Law means all automotive vehicles (including chassis)
using internal combustion engines for motive power and
exceeding total cylinder displacement of 75o cubic
centimeters. The term automobile parts as used in
Article 2 of the Automobile Menufacturing Industry
Law means parts for such automobiles and is to be
further defined by later ordinance.
Article 2. The limit referred to in the first
paragraph of Article 3 of the Autmobile Manufacturing
Industry Law shall be, in the case of automobiles, three
thousand per year, and, in the case of automobile parts,
parts for three thousand automobiles per year.
Article 3. Any person desiring exemption from
the payment of income or business profit taxes under
the provisions of Article 6 of the Automobile Manu-
facturing Industry Law shall report to that office
of the Tax Bureau under the jurisdiction of which he
resides, and state the amount of his income or busi-
ness profit in accor&ance with the provisions of
Article 24 of the Income Tax Law or of Article 1l of
the Business Profit Tax Law; and if he receives an
additional income or business profit from enterprises
other than those for which he seeks exemption from
payment of income tax or business profit tax, he shall
affix to the said report a statement of accounts re-
lating to the additional income or business profit
and a separate statement of accounts relating to the
income or business profit from the said enterprises
themselves.
Article 4. Any person who has been engaged in,
or is at the time engaged in, an enterprise for which
he seeks exemption from the payment of income or busi-
ness profit tax under the provisions of Article 6 of
the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law may be granted
an extension of the period of exemption from the payment
of such taxes if the said period be not expired. When
any person who has operated an automobile manufacturing
company or companies obtains a further license under the
terms of Article 3 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law, the period of exemption accorded to the
earlier enterprise or enterprises shall be taken into
account in applying the provisions of Article 6 of the
same Law.
Artiole 5.

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Article 5. Tools, machines, or materials which
may be exempted from the imposition of import duties
under the provisions of Article 8 of the Automobile
Manufacturing Industry Law are necessary articles for
the automobile manufacturing industry and shall be spe-
cified by subsequent Ordinance.
Article 6. Any person desiring exemption from the
imposition of import duty in accordance with the pro-
visions of Article 8 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law, shall affix to each import declaration
import permit issued by the Minister of Commerce and In-
dustry.
The import declaration mentioned in the foregoing
paragraph shall be made in the name of the automobile
manufacturing company.
Article 7. If articles exempted from the imposition
of import duty under Article 8 of the Automobile Manu-
facturing Industry Law are not, within three years from
date of importation, used for the purposes for which im-
ported, import duty shall be imposed on such articles.
The foregoing provision shall not govern when articles
are applied, within three years from date of importa-
tion, to other purposes or enterprises which may be erempt
from the imposition of import duty and permit of the
Minister of Commerce and Industry to this effect
is produced and reported to the ustoms authorities con-
cerned. The period mentioned in the first paragraph
may be extended when the Minister of Commerce and In-
dustry deems it necessary.
Supplementary Rules.
The present Ordinance shall be enforced from the day
on which the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law shall
be enforced.
The provisions of Articles 5,6 and 7 shall apply when
import duty is waived under the provisions of paragraph
6 of the Supplementary Rules of the Automobile Manu-
facturing industry Law

Ordinance No. 6 of the Department of Commerce
and Industry.
The following are hereby made the regulations for the
enforcement of the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law.
July 10, 1936.
Gotaro Ogawa, Minister of
Commerce and Industry.
Regulations for the Enforcement of the
Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law.
Article l. Automobile parts as referred to in the
second sentence of Article 1 of the Enforcing Ordinance
for the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law are the
following:
(1) Engines, cast or finished cylinder blocks,
finished crank shafts.
(2) Clutches.
(3) Transmissions.
(4) Differentials.
(5) Pressed steel body parts.
(6) Chassis.
Article 2. Any person desiring to obtain the license
referred to in Article 3 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law shall apply in writing to the Minister of Com-
merce and Industry and shall set forth in the application
the following information:
(1) Location of factory.
(2) Quantity of products to be manufactured or
assembled, classified according to type of
automobile and type of automobile parts.
(3) Equipment for manufacture or assembling.
(4) Capacity of manufacture or assembling.
The application shall be accompanied by the papers mentioned
hereunder:
(1) Documents estimating the date of commencement
of

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
of operation
(2) Documents stating the degree of progress in
manufacturing or assembling.
(3) Doouments outlining the source of materials
or parts used.
(4) Prospectus of manufacturing or assembling
operations, accompanied by plans .
(5) Estimate of expenditure invested in the enterprise.
(6) Statement of the total funds invested in the enter-
prise and of means of obtaining required funds.
(7) Prospectus of sale of finished goods.
(8) Estimate of income and expenditure.
(9) Articles of Association, register, balance sheets,
inventories, business reports, profit and loss
accounts, distribution of profits, and list of
shareholders.
(l0)Documents attesting the qualifications of appli-
cants under the terms of paragraphs (1) and (2) of
Article 4 of the Automobile Manufacturing Industry
Law.
(1l) Documents outlining incident&al enterprises if the
applicant is engaged in incidental enterprises
other than automobile manufacturing.
Article 3. A permit shall be obtained from the Minister of
Commerce and Industry to cover any change in the information
referred to in the first paragraph of the preceding Article,
and the application shall state the reason for the change.
Article 4. Commencement of operations by an automobile
manufacturing company shall be reported immediately to the
Minister of Commerce and Industry.
Article 5. With regard to automobile manufacturing companies
the Minister of Commerce and Industry shall give public notice
of the following:
(1) Company's name, location of head office, and date
of issue of license, whenever a license is issued
under Article 3 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law.
(2)
The company's name, location of head office,
and cause and date of invalidation of license
whenever a license issued under Article 3 of the
Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law is in-
validated under the provisions of the third
paragraph of Article 4 or the third paragraph
of Article 5 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law.

(2) Parts and accessories of tools or machines covered

Prime movers and accessory appliances imported
for integral use with machines provided for in (1).
Article 7. Any person desiring to obtain the exemption
provided for in Article 8 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law shall apply in writing to the Minister of Commerce
and Industry, and the application shall include the following
information:
(1) Description, type, capacity, nature, quantity and
value of goods to be imported.
(2) Object of intended importation.
(3) Names of manufacturer and exporter.
(4) Necessity for importation.
(5) Port of arrival and erpected date of arrival.
The sanction of the Minister of Commerce and Industry
is necessary in the event of alteration in any of the foregoing
data.
Article 8. Any person who has imported goods under
permit provided for in Article 8 of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law shall immediately report the fact to the Minister of
Commerce and Industry setting forth the following information
accompanied by copy of import permit:
(1)

(1) Description and quantity of goods imported.

Article 9. Any person desiring the permit provided for
in Article 7 of the Enforcing Ordinance for the Automobile
Manufacturing Industry Law shall apply in writing to the
Minister of Commeroe and Industry and the application shall
set forth the following information accompanied by a copy of
the import permit:
(1) Description and quantity of the goods and new use
to which the goods are to be applied.
(2) Purpose of the new use of the goods.
(3) Reason for applying the goods to a new use.
(4) Date and port of importation.
Article 10. When goods imported free of duty under the
provisions of Article 8 of the Automobile Manufacturing In-
dustry Law have been employed for the object for which imported
the fact shall be immediately reported in writing to the
Minister of Commerce and Industry and the report shall contain
the following information and be accompanied by copy of the
import permit:
(1) Description, quantity, and object of use of the
goods imported.
(2) Date of using the goods for the said purpose.
(3) Date and port of importation.
When goods imported duty free under the provisions of
Article 8 of the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law cease
to be used for the object for which imported, that fact shall
be immediately reported in writing to the Minister of Com-
merce and Industry. The report shall state the reason there-
for, supply the information called for in sub-paragraphs
(1) and (3) of the preceding paragraph, and be accompanied by
a copy of the import permit.

to December 31 in advance, and shall apply to the Minister

cation for this purpose shall be made in writing not later
than October 31.
Article 15. The application referred to in the preceding

(5) Source of parts and materials.
(6)
Quantity of parts and materials used and on

(7) Estimate of revenue and expenditure.

Article 27. Persons entitled to engage in the

mit to the Minister of Commerce and Industry a statement

of enforcement of the present regulations, and the state-
ments shall be accompanied by documents containing the
following information:
(1) Location of factory on August 9, 1935,
and at the time that the present regu-
lations take effect.
(2)
Description of assembling and manufactur-
ing equipment on August 9, 1935, and at
the time that the present regulations
take effect, accom anied by a plan of
the same.

to submit the statement provided for in the first and second
paragraphs, the Minister of Com:erce and Industry shall fix
the quantity of automobiles and parts assembled and manu-
factured by them during the three years ending August 9,
1935.
Article 28. The scale of business conducted previous
to August 9, 1935, as referred to in the fourth paragraph
in the Additional Rules of the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Law shall be determined by the …inister of Commerce
and Industry on the basis of the quantity of production
arrived at in accordance with provisions of the preceding
article.
When the Minister of Commerce and Industry has made
the determination referred to in the foregoing paragraph
with regard to the persons named in the first paragraph
of the preceding article, he shall give public notice of
their names or firm names, places of business, and scale

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
JAPAIMSE AUTOLOTIVE ILDUSTRY
Introduction
The approach of the Japanese to the establishment
NOT FORB CATION
of a purely native automotive industry is not based
upon economics but upon the opinion of the Japanese
Government that reasons of national defense require
such a step regardless of the cost. To this must also
be added the fact that national pride is involved.
Automobiles are an indispensable necessity to the
expanding military forces.
Motor cars and busses are indispensable as
auxiliary means of transportation - trucxs and
delivery vans for fectories end business houses,
busses which feed the municipal railways, and taxis
for a very linited pert of the populetion. Busses
are also the only rapid means of transportation in
outlying districts Where it is not economically
feasible to build railways.
When the above outlets ere exhausted, there is,
practically speakin, no other market for sutomobiles.
Taking the present annual consumption of approximetely
40,000 automobiles of all kinds, exclusive of the
midget cars and three wheeled delivery vens, the
problem of the Japanese is first, to meet Army needs
and second, to produce passen er cars and trucks for a
small market at a price which the market cen afford,
and which will compete in efficiency and design with
American

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.97)
-2 -
American automobiles. As merican automobiles have
long since driven European cars fron the Japanese
market the issue is strictly between Amer icen and
Japanese producers.
Because of the lov: value of the Japenese currency
in international exchange end because of the relatively
low cost of labor in Japan the Japanese have been able
to accomplish a great deal in certain lines of manu-
facture, particularly steple products such us textiles.
But the manufacture of automobiles in volves other factors
in which Japan is at a distinct disadvantage. a iven
car sells in co petition with other cars only if it has
the same efficieney, durability, and odern desim that
the others have. Its me nurecture rey uir es heavy cutlay
in plant equipment, en ineering staf", and in e peri-
mental worx. Improvements in desi are bound to coe
principally from the place where they are mostly used
and produced. he lostion is therefore: cn the
Japanese, either wit! or vithout Government assistance
to their infent automobile industry successfully manu-
facture with  ut the background the United States has,
and from which the A rican au tomobile has developed.
Japanese

- 3 -

surrounds such metters, but for purposes of co parison
these figures siven below are ade uete. is stated in
the article (Enclosure Ho. l3 herewit!) in the Japan-

the motor-cer trade in Japan is divided into truck
chassis and the taxi service." as the evera e fa ily
in Japan earns only about Yen 100.00 (approximetely
50.00 U.3. currency) a  onth, a otor cer such as a
Ford or Chevrolet which costs over Yen 3000.00 or even
a Datsun midget car which costs over Yen 1500.00 is
entirely out of reach. The consumers &re linited to
the Japanese military forces, other Government offices,
business houses, trans por tation services and taxicab
operators, or, to quote The Japan Times and Lail (En-
closure No. 1o - "Automobile Inaustry In Japan Growing
Gradually"), "in Japan there is only one motor car for
every 1,300 persons."
Classifying  otor car consumers into (a) Govern-
ment, business and taxicab owners and (b) private own-
ers, it can be reasonably expected that class (a)
owners

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
owners will ∞ntinue to provide only a gradually in-
creasing market except in time or war. (see pages 13-14
of this report for military demand for automobiles.)
So fer as business ana industry are concerned, the
tremendous expansion which hes teken place in Japan
since the Great lar has begun to show signs of coming
to the level of diminishing returns. It is true that
expansion into Manchuria and China is epparently still
in progress and the heavy and increasin budgets for
the military forces indicate that the Government will
make increased purchases of motor cars. But in the
long run it does not seem reasonable to suppose that
class (a) will ever be sufficient to support an entire
automobile industry which can compete in price with
American irports.
Class (b) owmers at present represent only 5% of
the pessenger car buyers, which means that only about
4,Ooo passenger cars are ovmed by private individuals
in Japan. (This does not include owners of Datsun and
other midget cars.) It is herdly necessary to say that
such a market is no market at all for en autonobile
industry. The only reasonable justification for
establishing such an in dustry for such buyers, even
with the heaviest Government backing, would be that a
remarkable increase in purchasing power is expected in
a fairly near future. But there is no reasonable hope
that such an increase is going to take place for many
years to come. It is only necessary to remember that
the

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
the extremely low wages of the Japanese laboring and
"white-collar" classes are the most important reason
for Japan's remarkable competitive position in inter-
national trade to understand that the Japanese Govern-
ment and ruling business families have no intention
of raising the general wage scele to any eppreciable
extent.
Higher wages would wipe out Japan's inter-
national trade. Without hi her wages there can be
no important increase in the number of private ovmers
of automobiles, even of the midget cars.

Enclosure No. 1
MEMORANDUM
OF VICE CONSUL LUDDEN
Yasushi Hatamura and Kyoichi Harashina were
granted visas under Section 3(2) on May 19, 1936.
Both are engineers in the employ of the Nissan
Jidosha Kabusniki Kaisha and are proceeding to the
United States in connection wi th the plans of the
Nissan Company to commence production of a different
type of motor car than the one now produced.
Both men expect to stay in the United tates
for about four months at the Graham-Paige plant at
Detroit. They stated that the des igns for the new
car are being made in the United itates and the first
motor was built at the Graham-Paige plant and is now
being tested.
Mr. A. N. Little is et present in the United
States purchasing machinery. It is expected that
production of the new car will commence about the
end of this year. At present it is planned to
produce a passenger car about the size of the Ford
V 8 and a truck of approximately lt ton capacity.
Both men stated that they wo uld wi tness the
tests of the motor for the proposed new car, but
their primary object in going to the Uniteu tates
was to study American production methods in the
automotive industry. They will be in cherge of
production at the Nissan plant upon their return
to Japan.

Enclosure No. 2
MEMORANDUM
Conversation Between Mr. Thomas Belsjoe of
Chrysler Corporation and Mr. Merrill
May 30, 1936.
So far as the conversation could be directed,
it was toward ascerteining Mr. Belsjoe's opinion on
the factors preventing Japanese motor car producers
from offering substantial competition to Ameri can
automobiles. Something was said, however, about the
new automobile control law which has just been en-
acted, a copy of which with translation, Mr. Belsjoe
then offered to supply.
In connection with the law and its effects, Lr.
Belsjoe pointed out that the assignment of basic quota
numbers to Ford, General Motors, and Chrysler had not
yet been effected but might be in the ratio respe ctively
of, for example, 9:7.5:5; that its interpretation and
application would be more important in its effects
than the law itself; that he had some hopes of raising
the fraction allowed to the Chrys ler Export Corporation;
and that one of the most important espects of the law
so far as American exporters a re concerned is the ap-
plication of the additional 50% tariff on any import-
ations over the eventual quots. For example, Ford
might import the allotted number of, say, 9,óo0 units
under the present tariff rate of 35% but additional
units would be dutiable at 85%.
Illustrating the difficulties which importers
have to meet, he mentioned the law enacted some three
years ago requiring a 2l-inch clearance. He had then
told the Chrysler Corporation to al ter its frame cast-
ings a ccordingly, but found that in a few months fremes
with the requisi te clearance were being manufactured
in Japan more cheaply than they could be exported from
America. On his recommendation frames were then not
included in the exported units.
The reported negotiations of Nissan wi th Graham-
Paige for the design of a larger car than the present
Datsun, could be ignored, even if they were still
ostensibly being carried on. They already possessed
designs for a motor similar to the v8 which they ∞uld
manufacture at any time that it appeared desirable to
do so. (See Enclosure No. 1 and Enclosure No. 4, third
page.)
Competitive

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
38
- 2 -
Competitive Position.
Japanese plants could not possibly produce an
automobile which could compete with American cars in
an open market; but it should be remarked that the
analysis of this inability to compete, which is made
below, is hypothetical, because, having been adopted
into the family of strategic industries, self-suffi-
ciency and not "the buyer's money's worth" is the
object. The Japanese automobile industry is an ideal
case for those who oppose whet has been called "the
autarchy of economic self-containment."
The Japanese industry has neither the advantage
of vertical independence nor the economies of mass
production. That is to say, its raw materials must
be imported in most instances, and, in all instances,
transported a great distance.* secondly, the product
in an output of 2,7ol units could not, even if the
iron and alloy metals and coal gravitated into the
plant from the sky, contain the intrinsic value of
the automobile which is part of en output of 3,000,000
units. The effort and expense necessary to the obtain-
ment of raw materiels and the disadvantages felt,
particularly in the automobile industry, of small
scale of production ren ered economic competition
impossible.
There are other factors including research in
automotive and machine tool desin. The wind tunnel,
field tests, and a staff of draftsmen working on
models that will not see service on the road for a
season or two, are unknown in Japan. The automotive
improvements and machine tool inventions which result
from American initiative are said by Mr. Belsjoe to
be installed in Japan -- within the limits imposed
by the ratio of their cost to available funds -- as
and when either the designs are made available by
the owners, or the car incorporating them reaches
the market in Japan. While the products of research
are therefore all "imported," the time lag between
their introduction and manufacture in America and
their manufacture for use in Japan is therefore at
least one season. The model obtained from the im-
ported car is said to constitute the basis for patent
applications made by Japanese. The applications are
said to be made and patents to be granted after the
requirement for public notice has been fulfilled by
advertisements in inconspicuous newspapers which
seldom, if ever, come to the attention of observers
in the larger cities. Since the Japanese in dustry
appears able to utilize research in this way, the
absence of laboratories in the industry of Japan
appears
This statement is substantiated by the estimate
previously reported that in the drop forge depart-
mont of the Nissan factory, costs may be roughly
divided into labor:l/59, materials:30/59, over-
head:28/59. (See page 4 in despatch No. 105 of
July 11, 1935.)

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
39
- 3 -
appears to be only a secondary factor in the competitive
weakness.
Japanese workers are said to be efficient but not
speedy. Their precision is excellent after the blue
prints have been studied and a few trial operations
performed. When machinery breaks down, repair work
takes time and resourcefulness depends upon experience.
Their normal hours are from 7 to 10:30, 10:55 to 12:00,
1 to 3:30, and 3:55 to 6:00. A mechanic earns Yen 5.00
a day, plus overtime, which Mr. Belsjoe said was too
variable to sum up in a few figures. It is both er-
roneous and misleading to attempt to draw a statistical
comparison between this wage and that of the American
worker. The Japanese worker is provided with rice by
the company and at least semi-annual bonuses. He is
practically assured of continued employment and a
pension under the law. He is epperently content with
the real wage thus earne ana since his family is
more important than his Union, manufacturers experience
few organized interferences by labor organizations.
Apart from factory and plent operations, the
alloys from which tools ana pe rts are menufactured
depend again on laboratories for i provements. In
this field there is elso a time lag.
The above description relates priarily to the
power unit of automobiles, but &lso to dvences in
casting, stamping, etc.
The automobile owner of Japan finds thot parts
for replacement are made largely in Jepan. itandard
gears, shefts, joints, axles, shims, clutch facings,
nuts and bolts, wheels, spokes, hub caps, fenders and
other accessories are locally made, as are upholstery
and interior furnishings. There are still a few
exceptions sucn as ball anā roller bearings which are
all imported.

Enclosure No. 3
MEMORANDUM
OF CONVERSATION OF JUNE 2, 1936
WITH MR. S. YAMAMOTO, MANAGIG DIRECTOR
OF NISSAI JIDOSHA KABUSHIKI KAISHA
Mr. Yamamoto's replies concerned firstly the
progress on the local market of the present Datsun
car, secondly the company's plans with regard to
the construction of a larger car as reported by the
press and including the purchase of more American
machinery and the employment of American experts
to oversee the initial stages of production as was
done wi th the Datsun. He elso commented on the
anticipated competitive position of the larger car
and the possibility for the general expansion of
the automobile market in Japan.
Progress of the Datsun.
The company wo uld continue with the manufacture
of the small car, having found e steady market for
about 5000 units a year includin pa ssenger cars and
small delivery trucks which compete with the numerous
three-wheel motorcycle vans. Of the elivery type,
Mr. Yamamoto states that his company is making 3600
annually, leaving 1400 passenger cars completed yearly.
The former can compete successfully wi th the motorcycle
vans, seating two on the driver's seat with protection
from the rain, and having a le rger capacity. Mr.
Yamamoto states that monthly sales amount to 20oo in
Tokyo and Yokohama and loo in Osaka area and that
the 1ife of the passenger car is about 50,000 to
70,Oo0 miles or three years as compared with one year
for the delivery vans.
The Datsun has remained the same size although
a spare tire is on the back and a bunper on the front.
Since these two additions are removable by hand (wing
nuts), the car is still a "cycle car" under the law.
The Datsun plant hes expanded, 1600 men being
employed. Many are apprentices. It is difficult to
discharge men. Under government regulations concern-
ing pensions, the number of men must be kept fairly
constant.
The small passenger car market is not competitive.
That is, the Datsun is not, in general, an alternative,
from the purchaser's point of view, to the Tord,
Chevrolet, Plymouth. The larger car which Wissan
proposes

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
41
-2-
proposes to build would compete in their market, how-
ever.
The export of Datsuns hed been abandoned es a
failure.
Proposed Manufacture of Larger Car.
A car of the Ford-Chevrolet type would be made
by Nissan and with regard to the plant for its manu-
facture representatives were now in the United States
to purchase machinery and to negotiate with a company,
the name of which Mr. Yamamoto could not disclose,
for machine tools and to review the motor design for
possible improvements. Mr. Yamamoto stated that the
motor had been designed in Japan.*
The price of the new car, said Mr. Yamamoto,
would be less than that of the Ford, Chevrolet and
Plymouth, probably about Yen 3o00. Every attempt
will be made to construct a car which is up to date
in every respect.
With regard to the manufacture of the new car
and the construction of the plant, Mr. Yamamoto
said that it had not been decided how much of com-
ponent materials, and parts if any, would be im-
ported from the Únited States. For the plant a large
amount of machinery would be bought in the United
States. Mr. Yamamoto said, however, that the al-
ternative of purchases in Germany was a very definite
one in the minds of the directors if, "for patriotic
reasons," American manufacturers showed a reluctance
to sell.
Less machinery would be needed for the new plant
than had been bought for the present Datsun factory,
although all types of machinery were to be purchased
including lathes, milling machines, electric welders,
drop forges, presses, automatic die cutters, radial
driils, etcetera. While all the new machinery could
be obtained in Japan, he said, it was in such demand
by the heavy industries of Japan that a delay would
be suffered. Mr. Little**was now in the United tates
arranging for purchasers.***

Alloy steels would be imported in increasing
amounts, mostly from the United States.
Mr. Yamamoto stated that he could not discuss
any eventual royalty arrangements with American firms.
Three or four American experts will be employed
until production is well under way. American experts
employed in the past had always urged the purchase of
American machinery. Of these they had found large,
expensive machines, serving only one or two operations,
to be uneconomical. German machinery salesmen had
been concerned about this and Mr. Gaulard, who re-
presented French "Solex" carburetors had been received
favorably by Nissan. They had recommended his con-
ferring with the Nissan Company's representatives in
the United States.
Prospects for Expansion of the Automobile
Market in Japan.
The present narket for cars of the Ford-Chevrolet-
Plymouth type is considered to be from 20,000 to 30,000
units a year. Of their proposed ler ger car Nissan
plans production of 5000 the first year, 12,000 the
second year, and 24,000 the third year, accordin to
Mr. Yamamoto's statement.
He seemed to be under the impression that quota
numbers under the Automobile Control Law recently
enacted would not* be assigneu until Nissan was
producing its larger car in quantity. Ford, Ceneral
Motors, and others would only be curtailed under
the law gradually so as not to disturb the market.
The Government "and those interested in Japan
becoming self-sufficient in automobile production"
realize the importance of road buildinc, improvement
and maintenance. The impression was gained that al-
though he said this was true, reelization by the
military of the importance of roads does not mean that
they will urge -- or oven approve -- the diversion
of funds for this purpose.
It is possible that eventual expansion of demand
will allow Nissan as well as Ford, General Motors,
Chrysler, etc. (under quotas) to continue in business
without loss to them from present scale of operations.
Roads
*See Memorandum or Conversations with Mr. Belsjoe
who stated quotas would be assigned in August 1936.

Roads are under the Ministry of Comnunicstions

He did not feel that the latter could or would actively
oppose road building if it were advocated by the former.
Much freight is already carried long distances
in Japan by truck, but figures - he said - are not
kept.

Enclosure No. 4
MEMORANDUM
OF CONVERSATION OF JUNE 8
WITH MR. THOMAS BELSJOE
OF THE CHRYSLER EXPORT CORPORATION
Mr. Belsjoe brought to the Consulate a copy of
the "Excerpts from the Automobile Control Law" (En-
closure No. 6 ) and reported on the true nature of
the negotiations between the Nissan Jidosha Company
and Graham-Paige. He also brought a copy of the
catalogue of the Toyoda Truck Company, large suppliers
of the army and almost certainly heavily subsidized
by the army and navy.
The Aim of Self-Sufficiency in the Heavy
Industries and Its Effects on the Importer
of American Automobiles.
With regard to the Automobile Control Law, Mr.
Belsjoe stated that quotas were expected to be
assigned in August 1936. The Law had been "in
effect" since August 9, l935 although it had only
been enacted in May 1936. Those who sought self-
sufficiency at any cost in automobile production
visualized an independence in truch manufacture in
ten years. The law was n useful and elastic instru-
ment for effecting the realization of that end. The
"loopholes" which made it elastic might be exploited
by a person in Mr. Bels joe's position who resigned
himself to doing honor to those really behind the
movement of industrial indepen dence. But anyone,
including the Ford Company in Japan, who objected
to their terms, wo uld find the "loopholes" closed
against him. On the other hand their terms were
bound to rest on their needs of the moment as well
as their aims.
The protagonists of adequate defense were not
interested in passenger cars, ninety-five percent of
which were taxis. They were concerned with the pro-
duction of trucks. The Toyoda Company was the most
important maker of trucks and no one knew how many
they supplied to the services. It was certain that
the Japanese army was less mechanized than the Russian
or the American army. For a standing army of 230,000,
three thousand trucks might be needed from Toyoda
annually. He had no way of arriving at the figure.
You could only guess.
The Toyoda truck is available to the public at
4500 yen. The real cost of production was doubtless
much higher; but if the compeny collected 5o0 yen
"profit"

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
45
- 2 -
"profit" per unit at this price, the company's subsidy
was equal to the di fference between the cost of production
per unit less 4000 yen, multiplied by the number of
units. The army probably paid 2000 to 2500 yen for
each truck it purchased. Merely as an illustration,
assume 30o0 cars a year for the army and a cost of
production of 8000 yen. 8000 less 4000 is 4000 yen
subsidy per unit. At 3o00 trucks per year the army
would pay the Toyoda Company 4000 times 3000 or 12,000,000
yen plus 3000 times 2500 or 7,500,000 yen, a total of
17,500,000 yen.
Whatever the figure, the company's capacity was
thus provided for and the price of parts made by it
or other subsidized firms was proporti onetely lowered
to the army.
The truck consists of Chevrolet-design motors,
Ford frames and axles, and Dodge transmissions and
cooling system -- all made in Japan, according to Mr.
Bels joe.
Given the materials, they can make any part in
Japan. Given the subsidy they can price it at a point
safely removed from the compe tition of the imported,
American-made product. The catch in the puzzie is in
(1) obtaining materials (up-to-the-minute alloys)
which will stand up, and (2) assuring the continuance
of the subsidy.
They take what they can get in matarials. The
subsidy depends on continuing to sell "the need for
defense" to the taxpayer. If sales resistance should
crop up in this field the motor car industry of Japan
would fall of its own weight. As long as subsidy funds
are forthcoming the ob ject of independence in produc-
tion is maintaineu, but at the sacrifice of all con-
siderations of profit. The Nissan could not float its
stock on the open market. The same ma rket had recently
balked at new loans for the South Manchurian railway
to the amount of Yen 8,000,O00. These cases are indices
to the resistance which is met when funds for "strategic"
undertakings must be raised tnrough normal financial
channels. The subsidy funds on the other hand, are
collected from the taxpayer and statistics on the
amount which go es to industry are unavailable. Since
most taxpayers, in contrast to the bankers, are not
concerned with, or are not in a position to judge,
the economic wisdom of government spending, industry
may get its subsidies long after the investing public
turns down its share offerings.
Mr. Belsjoe feels, therefore, that the only
reasonable attitude for a salesman in his position is
to

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
46
-3 -
to seek his Chrysler purchasers in those quarters
where funds are controlled. It so happens, he says,
that direction of the policy of independence for the
heavy industries rests in the same hands. From his
point of view praising the policy costs nothing and
as long as the aim falls short of realization the
opportunities for his sales continue. No advantage
can possibly be gained by voicing resentment at the
measures taken. They may appear to be injurious to
American trade. They doubtless are for the moment.
But the uncertainties include the prospeot of hostil-
ities in which case demand would rise. It is also
not impossible that revenue will fall off,and with
it,subsidies, in which case domestic automobiles could
offer less competition than ever to imports.
Negotiations Between Nissan and Graham-Paige.
Mr. Belsjoe had just learned that Nissan had
sent engineers to the Graham-Paige plant to purchase
obsolete machinery to be used in the production of
their projected larger car. He had been co rrect in
believing that they co uld not be negotiating for
motor or car designs.(See Enclosures No. 1 and
No. 2.)
Diesels.
The Diesel Truck now advertised by Mitsubishi
was taken, Mr. Belsjoe felt, from a representative
western model. It could not offer competition to
gasoline engine trucks because although its fuel
consumption might be low, its operation was un-
certain in that replacement of parts was a high
expense in the operation of Diesel trucks even when
they are driven by a skilled mechanic. His company
was not concerned over any competition offered in
this market by Diesel-powereu trucks or buses.

Enclosure No. 6
Excerpts - New Law for the Advancement
of Automobile Industry in Japan
Application for the production of an increased
number of automobiles or parts than allotted may be
made only by a company whose capital, shareholders
and directors are over 50% Japanese.
Five years' exemption from income and all taxes
are allowed to those companies which have been given
permission to operate.
Similarly, all tools, machinery and materials
imported will be exempt from import duties during
five years from the effective date of the new law.
In the event that imported cars or parts cause
interference with the progress of domestic products,
the Government reserves the right to inpose additional
duty not exceeding 50%.
In the event of any new porty starting operation
without securing permit, a fine will be imposed, not
exceeding Yen 5000. Directors of such a party will
likewise be fined, uo to Yen looo. per person.
Any company which has been in operation up to the
9th of August l935 may continue operation without
securing a permit within the limit of its pro duction
up to the date stated. In case, however, its produc-
tion is found to interfere with the advancement of
domestic products, a limit shall be set.
NOTE
In reply to questions put before the
House of Peers by certain Party representetives
on behalf of Automobile and Parts Dealers,
assurance was made that careful consideretion
would be given to the import of automobiles
and parts in case restriotion of such by im-
position of additional tariff or other means,
causes incovenionce to the users of automobiles
such as to affect their livelihood.

Enclosure No. 8
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE
WEEKLY COMMERCIAL SUPPLEMENT
MAY 7,1936
AUTO INDUSTRY BILL
Government To Have Full
Control Of Makers
The Department of Connerce and Industry will
introduce in the coming session of the Diet a bill
providing for control of the motor car industry. It
has already secured the Cabinet's consent.
In a statement issued immediately after the
Cabinet Council on Tuesday (28th), when final decision
was reached, the Department of Commerce and Industry
says that the establishment of the motor car industry
is necessary not only from the national defence point
of view but also from the point of view of industrial
activity. The latest situetion both at home and abroad,
in fact, renders the need i perative.
The Government seeks mass production of cors, so
that the country may be independent of foreign makes.
The ist of the new bill is as follows:--
Manufacturers of cars and parts, as stipulcted
by the Government order, shall obtai the permission
of the Government.
Applicants must form a joint stock company, more
than haif the share capital end voting rights belong-
ing to Japanese subjects or Jepanese corporations.
(a) Any authorised manufacturing concern shall
enjoy remission of taxes for & certain fixed period.
(b) Any authorised manufacturing concern shall
be given special facilities in connectior: with an
increase of capital, or issue of a loan.
Matters relative to public interest.
The Government shall be authorised to issue to
manufacturing concerns such orders es are necessary
in the interests of the public, and in connection with
official surveillance.
The Government shall be authorised to issue such
orders as are necessary from the national defence point
of view.
Importation

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.98)
54
- 2 -
Importation of cars and parts.
The Government is authorised to restrict imports
of cars and parts, or impose an additional import duty
on such imports, in case imports or a fall in prices
is liable to hamper the establishment of the motor car
industry in Japan.
A special committee called the Motor Car Industry
Committee shall be appointed to study permits for manu-
facturing and other important questions relative to the
working of the measure.
The transitional regulations provide that manu-
facturers 1n existence before August 9th, 1935, or
their successors, shall be ellowed to continue busi-
ness wi thout obtaining the pe rmission of the Government,
provided they are actually en gaged in manufacture at
the time of enforcement of the measure.

Enclosure No. 9
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE
WEEKLY COMMERCIAL SUPPLEMENT
MAY 7,1936
GASOLINE PRICE INCREASE
Five Sen Rise Permitted
By Commerce Department
Oil traders have secured the consent of the Depart-
ment of Commerce and Industry to raise their gasoline
quotation by five sen a gallon. The new retail price
will be 5l sen per gallon, to be put in force on the
7th instant.
In approving the increase, Dr. Ogawe, the Commerce
Minister, pledged traders against a further rise even
in case of a tariff increase. It may safely be con-
cluded therefore, that there will be no further price
increase for the time being.
In a statement issued on riday (lst), the Depart-
ment of Commerce and Industry said:-- "As the result
of extensive investigations, the Government agrees that
the recent rise in crude oil prices, as well as in
freights rates, renders their business extremely dif-
ficult, and that a price increase is inevitable.
Convinced, however, thet there is still room for
rationalisation, it has decided to allow an increase
of only five sen."
The statement added thet in addition to the pledge
not to request a price increase in case of the project-
ed tariff increase, traders have promised a reduction
should there be a fall in crude oil prices.
The Department of Commerce and Industry naturally
knows the present price increase will affect taxi and
truck operators. In its opinion, however, the dif-
ficulty can be overcome through "a rationalisation."
It is largely from this conviction of officials of
the Commerce Department, the Home Office, the Railways
Department and the other Departments in terested.
It is stated that oil traders are not fully
satisfied with the present price increase, but because
of the public's reaotion accepted the limit suggested.
Taxi operators are up in arms, reports the Japa-
nese press.
In Tokyo, the Automobile Union held an emergenoy
meeting in the Marunouchi building on Saturday (2nd
inst.). To-day they will hold a director's meeting,
inviting taxi operators' representatives from Osaka,
Kyoto, Nagoya, Kobe and other cities in Western Japan.
An opposition movement will be launohed.

MEMORANDUM OF
CONVERSATIONS WITH MR. BENJAMIH KOPF,
GENERAL MANAGER OF FORD
MOTOR CONPANY OF
JAPAN
Many conversations have taken place between Mr.
Kopf and myself during the past two years regarding
the automotive industry in Japen, most of which have
been reported in previous communications to the
State Department. I have attempted to convey the
sense of his remarks accurately wherever he is
referred to in this report and it is not believed
necessary to make a fur ther itemized list of his
opinions.

Enclosure No. 13
JAPAN-MANCHOUKUO YEAR BOOK, 1936
PAGES 448-451
AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY
Spread of the Use of Automobiles.
It was in l902 or two years before the Russo-
Japanese War that automobiles were first imported into
Japan. But it is since the great earthquake of 1923 in
the Kwanto district, which was followed by great im-
provements in the roads and high ways in not only the
area affected but all over the country that the use of
automobiles has become very popular. Especially notable
is the growing popularity of the taxi cabs since the
earthquake. Motor buses are al so increasingly favoured
by the public in preference to street cars. As a result,
especially in Tokyo and Osaka and ot her large cities,
the bus service is gradually expanding with occasional
reduction in fares.
Motor trucks are being more and more utilized,
especially for interurban and country-urban hauls of
produce, and the tendency is in favour of heavier
trucks. Truck sales, however, are being cut into a
good deal by three-wheeled motorcycle rear-vans, which
are very popular for small freight transportation in
cities.
In contrast to their growing increase in the motor
service for the public, motor-cars for pers onal use
are still quite limited in number, occupying only a
few per cent. of the number of passenger cars. More
than 90 per cent. of the pas senger cars in Tokyo are
estimated to be in the taxi class. In Osaka the figure
closely approaches loo per cent. Thus for practical
purposes, the motor-car trade in Japan is divided into
truck chassis and the taxi service.
National Production of Automobiles.
It was originally for military purposes that the
Government turned its attention to the encouragement
of the production of automobiles. In Merch, 19l8 a
law for subsidizing the manufacture of military motor-
cars was enacted. The law provided that the manufacturer
with a capacity of turning out lo0 or more six-wheeled
trucks of lt metric tons or more or buses should be
entitled to a subsidy, and that in time of war the
Army acquire the ri ght to requisition the vehicles thus
manufactured. Until a few years ago home made auto-
mobiles in Japan were almost confined to this class of
cars, and their number was quite limited. It was as
late

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
60
- 2 -
late as 1931 that the necessity of setting up the
motor industry as a civil enterprise was recognized
by the Government when an Automobile Industry Committee
was organized by the Department of Conmerce and Industry.
At that time it was observed by the Committee that the
motor industry would pay when it had attained a capacity
of turning out about 5,ooo trucks and buses a year.
A subsidy of Yen 130,O00 was itemized in the Budget
estimates for each of the financial years of 1932-33
and 1933-34 (Yen 300 per car on an average).

as shown by the returns of the Department of Commerce
and Industry, will be mted from the table appended:-

Compared with the preceding reer, the number of

This is due chiefly to the encouragement by the Army
and the Department of Commerce and Industry. But, the
number is still far less than is ailed at by the autho-
rities, or the 5,0o0 level as referred to above. On
the other hand, spectacular developments are being
shown by the manufacture of light otor-cars, as is
indicated by the fi rures tabulated above. It is
expected that in 1935 these cars will be turned out
to the number of 6,000, which is more than thirteen
times as large as that for 193?. The manufacture of
ordinary motor-cars is still far from competing with
imported vehicles, as may be gathered from the table
