The assemblage of cars with imported materials
is done at the Ford plant at Yokohama and at the
General Motors plant at Osaka. As will be noted from
the above figures, the number of cers thus assembled
reached the peak in 1929 at 18, 087 and began to pursue
a downward course from the following year. But, it is
expeoted that the number of cars imported in that way
in 1934 may have far superseded the 1929 level.
Of late there hes been an interesting new comer
to the market for the imported motor-cars, and that
is the German "Adler" car, a medium sized passenger car
with a front-wheel drive and an independent wheel
suspension.
Output of Parts.
The national production of some parts of the
vehicle has so much progressed that tyrea are already
exported to a considerable amount. The ou tput of
these parts was Yen 17,000,000 in 1931. The figure
increased to Yen 26,000,000 in 1932, and to Yen
36,000,000 in 1933. But, still a considerable portion
of the requirements of these articles has to be met
by imports.

The Tokyo Gas Electric Industry Co., Motor-car
Industry Co., and Mitsubishi Jyukogyo Kabushiki Kaisha
chiefly make trucks and pessenger cars, the Kawasaki
Vehicle Co., trucks and pas senger cars and buses, the
Japan Vehicle Co., passenger cars and the Nissan Motor-
car Co., and the Keisan Manufactory light cars.
The workshop of the Nissan Motor-car Co., at
Namamugi, Yokohama is the best equipped of the kind
and of the largest capa city. The vehicles turned out
thereat are known as "Datsuns." They are mostly light
passenger cars. Datsuns are already exported to
Australia and the South jeas.
The number of automobiles in use thr oughout the

of the total number of motor-cars of all sorts
now in use, it is estimated that 75 to 80 per cent. are
General Motors and Ford products, dividing the country,
taken as a whole, fairly evenly between these two great
organizations. The General Motors plant being located
at Osaka, there is naturally a leaning toward their
products in western Japan, while the same holds true of
Ford, whose plant is at Yokohama, in eastern Japan. If
anything there is a slight preponderance of General Motors
cars in use, for the reason that besides Chevrolets and
trucks, they put out a variety of other cars, with which
Ford can not complete. Moreover, General Motors have now
commenced to bring in their Opel cars made in Germany,
low-priced but of good appearance and performance, which
it is planned to assemble at the Osaka plant. The re-
maining 20 or 25 per cent. are largely represented by
Chrysler products and other higher priced American cars.
The status and achievements of the General Motors
and Ford plants in Japan, and their enormous influence
are too well known to mention. However, apart from the
important fact that they practically supply the motor
transport of the country wi th good and serviceable cars
at reasonable cost, the important contributions that
General Motors and Ford otherwise make directly to the
welfare of the country should be recorded, especially
in view of the campaign for "home products."
While

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.98)
5.5
- 5 -
While the engines, chassis, and certain other
parts are imported from America, large quantities of
tires, batteries, upholstery, glass, rubber equipment,
and other materials produced in Japan are used, to say
nothing of the labour employed.
General Motors and Ford have inaugurated instalment
payment system, as favourable as possible under the in-
covenient laws governing such matters in Japan, and have
found them to work out fairly satisfactorily. Losses
through default are reported at well under 5 per cent.
The instalment payment system is taken advantage of
practically only by those buying cars for taxi purposes.
The Bosch electrical equipment organization is one
of the notable features of the automo bile industry of
Japan. Bosch products and service reach every part of
the Empire and of Manchoukuo from the headquarters in
Tokyo and the branch service stations in Kobe, Nagoya,
and Fukuoka, through the countrywide netword of dealers.
The reasons for the outstanding success of Bosch
in a business so difficult bo th technically and com-
mercially are worth recording. The plan fact is, that
Bosch products are recognized in Japan as pre-eminent,
occupying the unique position of setting the quality
standard. This reputation has been built up by the
lifelong spirit of the now aged and revered founder,
Robert Bosch of Germany, as put into practice in Japan
by the agents C. Illies & Co., now established in Japan
for 76 years. Since the beginning of Bosch activities
in Japan the Bosch atch-word "service after Sale" has
been translated into actuality.
Starting with the electric outfits for the auto-
mobiles the Bosch products now cover and dominate the
mot orcycle and three-wheeler line as well, prove their
value in aeroplane ignition and Diesel engine equipments.
Recognizing the great future of automobiles trans-
portation, aviation and of the diesel engine in Japan,
new skillful engineers and experts from the Bosch works
have joined the staff of C. Iilies and Co., intensively
training technicians in both mechanical work and sales,
who now staff all the service stations and branches
in the Empire. It can be said without exaggeration that
the Bosch organization of C. Illies and Co., has proved
to be an indispensable medium for the whole automobile
industry of Japan and has widely cont ributed to its re-
markable progress during the past few years.
Control of the Automobile Industry.
On August 9, 1935 a draft bill for control of the
automobile industry that establishes a license system
under which foreign firm cannot do business unless they
let

- 6 -
let Japanese hold more than half of their shares was
submitted to the Cabinet by Mr. Machida, the Minister
of Commerce and Industry. The outline of the draft bill,
which was approved by the Cabinet and which is therefore
to be introduced into the next session of the Diet, is as
follows:-
(1) The assemblage of automobiles for the
masses or the manufacture of their prin-
cipal parts is to be placed under a
license system except in instances where
the quantity involved does not attain
the stipulateu volume. In such ins tances,
licenses will not be required. The pur-
pose of the system is to adjust demand
and supply and to prevent overproduction
and undue ∞mpe tition.
(2) The applicants for licenses must be
companies in which more then half of the
shares is held by Japenese sub jects. They
must also be established under the laws
of Japan, thus assuring that more than
half the rights of control are held by
Japanese subjects.
(3) Such enterprises as are licensed under
the foregoing provisions will be regulated
and controlled by appropriated means
from the s tandpoint of both industry and
national defense.
(4) The rights of companies that are already
established and fulfill the above require-
ments will be safe uarded, but companies
establishe after the promulgation of the
law or new installations by old companies
will be recognized retroactively vhen the
law pes into force.
In view of the international signi icance of the
measure, the Departnent of Commerce and Industry issued
a statement in the form of a talk with press represent-
atives, which was to the following effect:-
"The establishment of the automobile industry is
of great significance from the standpoint of present
and future national defense es well as industry in
general. As one of the basic industries, it occupies
an important position in the industrial field. Thus
the Government has for the past dozen or more years,
given encouragement and protection to its development.
In recent years, particularly, the need has arisen for
creating automobile menufacturing enterprises to supply
automobiles for the masses for which the demand has be-
come great. Recent defense and economic requirements
have

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.98)
65
-7 -
have a lso produced a demand for more au tomobiles.
"There are one or two enterprises that aim at turn-
ing out automobiles for the me sses on a considerable
scale, but they hardly merit the name of a national
automobile industry. Most of the influential automobile
assemblage enterprises in the country, moreover, are
foreign. Unless the Government ives proper guidance,
therefore, a national automobile industry cannot be
established without great difficulty. When Japanese
enterprises join hands with foreign companies, it is
necessary for the Japenese to control management rights.
"As mass production of automobiles designed for
the masses must be placed on a solid. besis, uncontrolled
establishment of numerous enterprises must be given
stability. Hence the various provisions in the draft
bill for the establishment of a domestic automobile
industry."
Foreign Firms Notified.

as follows:-
"As long as more then half the shares of companies
with foreign capital are held by foreigners, any ex-
pansion or new installations effected by them after
August 9, 1935, must be abolished when the law is en-
acted."

9

The Secretary of State,

Deparmer of Stat
Sir:
With reference to the Department's instruction No. 882,
dated November 18, 1935, in which was examined the question
of discrimination against American motor-car manufacturers
in Japan, and to subsequent correspondence (especially to
27
the Embassy's despatch No. 1659, dated February 5, 1936)
PTLHD
1/
on the same subject, I have the honor to enclose herewith
a copy of a memorandum prepared for the use of this Embassy
by the Ford Motor Company of Japan, Limited, describing
the restrictions which have been placed upon the Company's
activities
F/G

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
activities under the Automobile Industry Control Law.
Before proceeding to an examination of the memorandum
of the Ford Motor Company, it might be well to review briefly
the history of the problem. The Japanese Government (actually
the military) in 1935 decided to develop a domestic auto-
mobile industry and accordingly drafted the Automobile
Industry Control Law, which, however, was not approved
by the Diet until 1936. The law, when passed, was made
retroactive to August 9, 1935. It provided that any pro-
ducer of more than 3,000 motor-cars annually must be licensed
by the Government, and that licensees must be Jar anese sub-
jects, or, in the case of corporations, that the majority
of the stock must be held by Japanese subjects. Organizations
already producing automobiles in Japan, however, would be
allowed to continue operations "within the scope existing on
August 9, 1935". The Embassy believed the law to constitute
an infraction of Article l of the treaty of l9ll between the
United States and Japan, inasmuch as it restricted the operations
of American producers of automobiles in Japan, while per-
mitting Japanese producers full liberty of action. The
matter was referred to the Department, which authorized the
Embassy by instruction No. 882, referred to above, to discuss
the matter orally and informally with the Japanese Foreign
Office. This was done, the reply of the Foreign Office (re-
20
ported in despatch No. 1659 dated February 5, 1936) being
to the effect that it considered that Article l of the treaty
applied only to individuals and that as it was unlikely that
individuals would be licensed to manufacture automobiles in
Japan,

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Japan, no discrimination would occur. Corporations are
controlled by Article VII of the treaty, according to the
Foreign Office, and as such are subject to the laws and regula-
tions enacted in the countries in which they operate. The
stand of the Foreign Office, therefore, was that no question
of discrimination against the two American motor-car com-
panies (Ford and General Liotors) arose under the Automobile
Industry Control Law. Thus the matter stands at present.
The Ford Motor Company of Japan, Limited, points out
in its memorandum that it desired to construct a new plant
in Japan to operate within the scope of the Automobile
Industry Control Law; that it has been unable to obtain
permission to erect the factory; that it has not been allowed
to dredge a part of the basin adjoining its property and to
construct a loading dock; that it has been informed by the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry that it would not be per-
mitted to manufacture cars within Japan up to the limit of
3,Oo0 units per annum (for which no license under the law
is required); that it would not be allowed to manufacture or
to machine parts not covered by the law; and that it would
not be allowed under any circumstances to exceed its quota
of 12,360 cars per annum, even if Jananese producers could
not supply the demand.
These being the circumstances, the local representative
of the Ford Motor Company has requested the Embassy again to
examine the question in an effort to ascertain if another
protest can be filed with the Japanese Government on the
grounds of infringement of treaty rights. In view of the
fact that the Japanese Foreign Office has held that the
provisions of Article 1 of the treaty of l9ll apply only to
individuals,

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
individuals, it is difficult to see on what grounds
representations could again be made, unless the Depart-
ment deems it feasible to dispute the Japanese allegation
in regard to the scope of Article I.
The local representative of the Ford Motor Company
also desires that the Embassy make informal inquiries
of the Foreign Office as to the reasons for withholding
the building permit for the erection of its proposed
factory, which, it is pointed out, would operate within
the limits of the quota fixed by the law, except that it
might also manufacture under bond for the Chinese market.
The Embassy declined to take any steps in the matter
without the approval of the Department, and therefore
both questions are referred to the Department.
While the Embassy fully realizes the problems with
which the American motor-car companies are faced in Japan,
it cannot see that any good can be accomplished either
for the automobile interests or for the prestige of the
American Government by endeavoring to fight against the
present nationalistic trends in Japen. There is no doubt
that the Japanese military are determined to build up a
domestic motor-car manufacturing industry upon which
they can rely for motor transport in time of war, and no
amount of arguing with the Foreign Office on questions
of treaty rights will alter the determination of the
military. It would therefore appear to be the part of
discretion to accept the situation with the best grace
possible.
When the matter was presented to the local representa-
tive of the Ford Motor Company in the above light, he replied
that

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
that he realized that nothing could be hoped to be ac-
complished through diplomatic protests, but that he did
not wish to accept the situation without "putting up some
kind of a fight". Asked why he was so insistent upon
erecting a new factory at Yokohama when the old factory was
adequate for the business which the law would allow him
to undertake, he replied that there were various reasons
for the plan. One was his expectation that in course
of time the Company would be forced to sell out to Japanese,
and that the Company could obtain a much better price with
a modern plant than it could with an antiquated building
and obsolete equipment. Another was that the Company
desires to utilize a part of its Japan profits in erecting
the new factory, thus decreasing its book profits and
avoiding the higher brackets of the income tax and busi-
ness profits tax. Moreover, in the new factory the
Company would be able to manufacture more of the parts
of the car in Japan and would not only be able to make a
larger profit on the cars sold in Japan but could also
supply a part of the Chinese market from the Yokohama plant.
Despite the facts adduced above by the local repre-
sentative of the Ford Motor Company, the Embassy is still
of the opinion that it would be best for all parties con-
cerned to accept the conditions as they exist and not
to attempt to "swim against the current". The American
Government certainly would meet with a rebuff if it
attempted to interfere diplomatically with the plans of
the Japanese military, and it is difficult to see what,
if anything, the Ford Motor Company could hope to gain.
However

=== Page 250 === (confidence 0.93)
-6-
However, at the request of the local representative of
the Company, the matter is referred to the Department
for its consideration.
Respectfully yours,
()
Joseph C. Grew.

YOKOHAMA: Apri1 15, 1937.
STATEMENT FOR THE AMERICAN EMBASSY IN TOKYO
REGARDING THE ATTEMPT OF THE FORD MOTOR COM-
PANY TO EXTEND ITS ASSEMBLY PLANT IN JAPAN.
In 1934 the Ford Motor Company of Japan Ltd found it ad-
viseble to prepare to increase its assembling facilities at Yoko-
hama, the present plant having been inadequate for some time. An
extension of the old plant was considered, but realizing that
in the course of time it would be necessary to do more and
more manufacturing in this country and in any case it was
deemed advisable to install machining equipment for castings
and forgings which could be made locally but for which no
machining equipment was available, it was decided to build
an entirely new plant.
For this purpose, we intended to purchase a lo0-acre
site from the City of Yokohama. hile the negotiations were
proceeding, we were called upon and threatened by various
rightist organizations, who warned us to cease expanding.
Their threats were disregarded and negotiations proceeded
fairly satisfactorily, until the point was reached that the
deal could be completed. At that stage, the City autho-
rities were warned by the Japanese army not to sell the
land. We then looked for another site, and found a suitable
one belonging to the Asano interests. The usual threats from
rightist organizations were received by us and the Asanos,
and later the Army authorities approached the Asanos and
told them not to sell to us. After lengthy negotiations, the
owners of the land secured the understanding of the Army
and we purchased the site.
Soon after, in August 1935, the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry notified us that the Government had decided on a
basic policy for automobile manufacturing, that only organi-
zations having a majority of Japanese capital and with Japa-
nese management could be licensed, but that existing Com-
panies would be allowed to operate "within the scope ex-
isting as of August 9th 1935.
There being at the time no law to justify interference
with our plans, these were proceeded with, and eventually in
June l936 we applied for a factory permit and a building permit.
Up to date no decision has been rendered on these appli-
cations. Innumerable questions have been asked and data re-
quired, but we have been unable to get them to approve or
reject our applications. These have been modified at various
times to comprise only the equipment and operations we are
doing at the present factory, as we were told that RvoVAL was
quite permissible - with no result.
W。

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
We also applied for permission to build a loading
dock as part of our new plant, and to dredge part of the
basin adjoining our property. After nine months, we were
informed that the application could not be granted. The
authorities decline to tell us the reasons for the refusal.
They simply state that various Government departments had
reported on our applications and the majority were opposed.
In the course of various conversations with officials
of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry they were asked if
we could not manufacture within the quantitative limit of
3000 specified by the law, and for which no license is re-
quired. We were told we could not . Asked if we had not the
right to manufacture parts or to machine parts for which there
is no restriction by law, we were informed that we could not.
Asked if it was not true that any party had the right to do
these things which were not prohibited or limited by law, we
were told that it was so, but did not anply to the foreign
assembly plants, who by law were restricted to the operations
they were coing on August 9, 1935. Asked if this was not
discrimination, they informed us that there was No discrimi-
nation.
After the Automobile Control Law was passed and
regulations sanctioned, quotas were established for Ford
and General Motors. Our yearly quota was set at 12,360 and
for General Motors at 9,470. The Ministry of Commerce and
Industry was asked if we would be allowed to exceed this
quota in case the Japanese manufacturers should be unable to
fill the demand. e were informed that under no circumstances
would we be allowed to produce more tian 12,360 units in any
given quota year, even if the Japanese could not supply. They
were unable to say where the buyers would get the units.
In addition to hemming us in as explained above,
the authorities are preparing to restrict our business still
further by other means. Taxi, bus and truck operators are
being encouraged to form guilds, which are to receive assis-
tance from Government institutions on the understanding that
they will buy only Japanese cars and trucks. Bus operators,
who are dependent on franchises, are being "induced" to buy
Japan made units in order to get the franchise.
These facts are being submitted to you in view of the
interest you have manifested in this matter, to show how
American interests are being discriminated against in Japan,
possibly in violation of treaties existing between the United
States and this country. It would seem well for the American
Government to take note of this.
FORD MOTOR COMPANY OF JAPAN LTD
(Signed) B Kopf
Manager.

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 9, 1937
MMI
In this despatch from Tokyo the Embassy refers to
the Department, at the request of the Ford Motor Company
of Japan, the question of the right of the Company under
Japanese law to construct a new factory and to engage in
certain manufacturing operations. The Company desires that
the matter be examined with a view to determining if another
protest can be filed with the Japanese Government on the
grounds of infringement of treaty rights.
You will recall that in November 1935 the Embassy was
instructed to point out to the Japanese Government that
the restrictions on the manufacture of automobiles by
American citizens in Japan, contemplated at that time, would
be violutive of the Treaty. The Foreign Office declared in
reply that the provision of the treaty cited by the Embassy
is not applicable in the circumstances. There the matter has
stood until the present.
The Embassy is of the view that unless the Department
deems it feasible to dispute the Japanese allegation in
regard to the applicability of the Treaty, it would appear
difficult to see on what grounds representations could again
be made. In a memorandum dated February 10, 1936, Le stated
that the contention of the Foreign Office, of course, is
untenable, and advised that the Foreign Office be informed
that

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
that the proposed law is (would be) a clear violation of
the Treaty, and that the reasons advanced by the Foreign
Office are unacceptable to this Government. A draft
telegram to that effect for transmission to the Embassy
was prepared, but was not sent because of some doubt on
the part of this Division in regard to the legal principles
involved.
It would appear that the matter is at present in
exactly the same status as it was in May 1936, when the
draft instruction to the Embassy, above-mentioned, was
prepared and later laid aside. At that time Mr. Hornbeck
recommended to the Under Secretary "that we hold the
proposed instruction in reserve," presumably because of
the questionable legal basis of the Department's argument.
The Embassy is of the further opinion that no good
can be accomplished either for the automobile interests or
for the prestige of the American Governnent by endeavoring
to alter the determination of the Japanese military to
build up a domestic automobile industry by arguing with
the Foreign Office over treaty rights. The Embassy con-
siders that it would be the part of discretion for this
Government to accept the situation with the best grace
possible, and not to attempt to interfere diplomatically
with the plans of the Japanese military. In this opinion
I fully concur. Whatever the attitude of the American
Government toward this matter may be and however just the
contention

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
contention of the American interests concerned may be,
it seems certain that no amount of argument based on
legalistic considerations is likely to swerve the Japanese
Government from its determination to establish a domestic
automobile industry free from dependence on foreign capital
or foreign management. Common sense would therefore seem
to dictate that, in view of the fact that this Government
has already set forth to the Japanese Government its
attitude in regard to this matter, no further representations
be made at this time in the absence of a material change in
the situation.
In considering all aspects of this matter it is believed
that due weight should be given to the view of EA (Dr. Feis):
"I do not believe it to be to the American advantage to
exert ourselves to facilitate American capital and enterprise
for developing the automobile industry in Japan--the contrary
is true . . ."
There is appended an instruction to the Embassy stating
that in view of the circumstances the Department is not
inclined to tace up the matter with the Japanese Government
at this time.

Mr. Hamilton:
The contention of the Japanese Foreign Office,
as reported in the attached telegram from the Embassy
in Tokyo, is of course transparently untenable.
894.797/
Assuming, without discussing, the correotness of the
thesis of the Japanese Foreign Office that Article I
of the Treaty of l9ll applies only to individuals,
it necessarily follows that individual American citizens
C
C1
have exactly the same rights to manufacture automobiles
in Japan as are granted to Japanese subjects. If
Japanese subjects as individuals are not permitted to
operate automobile faotories but are permitted to do so
as Japanese corporations, compliance with the treaty
provision manifestly requires that American citizens
also be permitted to operate automobile factories as
Japanese corporations on a basis of exact equality with
1
all other Japanese corporations. Any other contention
would render meaningless and worthless the provisions
of Article I of the Treaty of l9ll which expressly
grants to American citizens "liberty * * * to own or
lease and occupy manufactories, warehouses and shops
and
7

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
and generally to do anything incident to or necessary
for trade upon the same terms as native * * * subjects"
of Japan. The Foreign Office's statement that the
position of "companies or corporations" is regulated by
Artiole 7 of the treaty and that Artiole I has no
application to the case, is not entitled to serious
consideration. Artiole 7 relates exclusively to the
status of the corporations of one of the signatory
powers doing business in the territory of the other
—1
signatory. It does not and obviously could not be
intended to refer to Japanese corporations in Japan or
American corporations in the United States. Since the
only American automobile factories in Japan are Japanese
corporations, it is evident that any discrimination
against them in favor of other Japanese corporations
would be a clear violation of the provisions of
Article I of the treaty of l9ll. That fact is undoubtedly
well known to the Foreign Office as is indicated in a
practical admission of its helplessness, despite the
feeble deolaration that it does not regard the proposed
law as a violation of the treaty. It is merely an
additional instance of a treaty obligation whioh, if
recognized, would prevent the Japanese from operating
"in the manner to whioh they have become accustomed".
The

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
The existence of the obligation must, therefore, be
denied, however overwhelming may be the evidence of its
reality.
In this si tuation it would seem to be useless to
attempt to enter upon a detailed discussion of the grounds
upon which our allegation of treaty violation is based.
If it is deemed necessary or desirable to disouss the
question further with the Foreign Office, I think we
should briefly and quite categorically declare that
the proposed law, if enacted, would be a clear violation
of Artiole I of the Treaty of l9ll, and that the reasons
advanced by the Foreign Office in denial of the treaty
obligation are so definitely contrary to the applicable
provisions of the treaty as to be unacceptable to the
Government of the United States.
894.797/18
Le:FXW:HWC:SS

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
18837
Febwuary 13, 1936.
C
Mr.Hamilton:
I entirely agree with Mr. Ward's memorandum of Febru-
394.797/34
ary 10, 1936, regarding the apparent contention of the
Japanese Government that Article I of the Treaty of 19ll
does not give American nationals the right to form corpora-
tions in Japan for the purpose of carrying on business in
that country. If the Japanese thesis were correct, it
would be possible to nullify the provisions of the treaty
merely by requiring that all business must be transacted
through corporations and by limiting or denying the right
of aliens to form such corporations or to participate in
their formation and operations.
As pointed out by Mr. Ward, Article VII of the treaty
has no bearing on the situation here in question since it
has to do only with activities in one country of corpora-
2EAL18 701
tions created in the other.
The precise question here presented was passed upon
by the Supreme Court of California May 20, 1927, in the
case of K. Tashiro et al. v. Jordan. Secretary of State,
et al.
F/G

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
et al. In that case Japanese nationals had prepared arti-
cles of inoorporation for the purpose of establishing a
hospital and had applied to the Secretary of State for a
certifioate of incorporation, which the latter had refused
to issue. The court required the issuance of the certifi-
cate stating, among other things, that the right "to carry
on trade" such as "conducting manufactories, warehouses,
and shops and leasing land: for commercial purposes, is
from the very terms of said treaty as extensive and complete
as that employed by native citizens or subjects".
The
court proceeded to state that "It would be difficult to
frame language much more comprehensive than that found in
* * article I of the tteaty" wherein it is provided
that the citizens or subjects of the High Contracting
Parties shall have liberty "to do anything incident to or
necessary for trade upon the same terms as native citizens
or subjects". The court further stated:
". . . It is hardly necessary to call attention
to the extent to which corporations are formed
and used by the citizens of this state, as well
as by those of all other civilized countries,
in the transaction of the various lines of trade
carried on therein. There is scarcely a class
of business of any consequence carried on in
this state in which corporate interests do not
play, if not a leading, at least an important
part in its transaction. The extensive and
widespread use of this means of carrying on
trade or transacting business is convincing
proof of the advantages to be derived therefrom.
Why, then, should this method of carrying on
trade as to the olasses of business enumerated
in the treaty be denied subjects of Japan
residing

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
residing in this state in the face of the plain
provisions of the treaty conferring upon them
as to such classes of business the right to do
anything incident to such business upon the same
terms as native citizens? A corporation, or the
members thereof, in conducting any certain busi-
ness do not thereby exeroise any more extensive
or different right or control over the property
of said business than they would exeroise over
said property if they carried on the business
as individuals."
The defendants in this case contended, like the
Japanese Government now contends, that Article vII negatived
any contention on the part of the Contracting Parties to
recognize the right to transact business through the
formation of corporations. As to this contention the
court stated:
". . . The parties to the treaty evidently
were not prepared to definitely bind them-
selves as to the rights of a corporation
organized in one country and domiciled
therein to transact and carry on business
in the other. Hence they left the question
unaffected by the treaty and subject to be
regulated by the respective countries. They
were, however, prepared to definitely fix
and determine the rights of citizens and
subjects of one country domiciled in the
other, and to this end they set forth these
rights distinctly and comprehensively in
article 1 of the treaty, the terms of which
we have already considered and attempted to
construe."(256 Pac. 545,548)
The situations referred to by Messrs. Vallance and
Murdock, in which the view was expressed that the rights
of American corporations should not be assimilated to
the rights of nationals, where nationals are referred to
in commercial treaties, are not here in point. Corpora-
tions

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
tions in those cases were those created in one country
which desired to have their status recognized in the
other country.
I think that a firm position should be taken by
the Department in this case. If the treaty is given
the interpretation contended for by the Japanese Gov-
ernment, it will be practically a meaningless instrument,
in so far as concerns trade and commerce, and should not
be given effect in this country, as has been done by
various decisions of our courts, including the one
referred to herein.
Le 894.797/18
GHH:AD

Automobile manufacture on

The automobile industry is one of the fundamental

not only in industry and transportation but also in

[Translation of enclosure to the above letter.]
ESSENTIAL POINTS OF AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY LAW.
1. Enterprises for the manufacture of principal
parts and the construction of ordinary automobiles
shall be licensed enterprises but permis sion shall not
be necessary where output does not exceed a specified
limit. The principle of licensing, taking into account
the demand and the supply, shall be to grant permission
only to one or more companies and not to grant permission
to others.
2. The organizations which are qualified to re-
ceive the aforementioned permission shall be limited
to corporations of which a majority of the stock is
owned by Japanese subjects, or to corporations which,
established as juridical persons under Japanese laws
and ordinances, are controlled in a majority of their
voting rights by Japanese subjects.
3. Regarding the automobile manufacturing industry
which has received the permission referred to in the
first paragraph, such supervision and regulations shall
be established as may be necessary for industrial and
national defense reasons.
4. As regards the already existing automobile
industry corresponding to the definitions of the first
paragraph, its rights and interests shall be recognized
within the limits existing at the time of the deter-
mination of this policy, (i.e. August 9, 1935), and the
accomplishment of such enterprises shali be permitted;
in regard to new undertakings established thereafter,
and extensions, such rights and interests shall not be
admitted when the law is hereafter enforoed.
The Japanese press and the English language press in

place the automobile manufacturing industry on a license basis.

THE JAPAN TIMES for December 28, 1935, carried the follow-
ing article:
LAW TO CONTROL AUTO PRODUCTION READY FOR DIET.
Strict Regulations Affecting Foreign Manufacturers
And Imports Drafted
The draft of the automobile manufacture law was
drawn up by the Department of Commerce and Industry
at the Departmental conference held Friday at the
official residence of the Minister of Commerce and
Industry. The draft will be submitted to the present
Diet session for approval.
Ever

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Ever since last year the Government has recognized
the necessity of controlling the manufacture of auto-
mobiles, and has been studying the question from all
sides, particularly with the view to fostering the
progress of the industry in Japan.
Main Points Given
The main points in the drafted regulation are as
follows:
1. Those desiring to manufacture automobiles
in Japan must obtain the Government's sanction. The
standard production capacity for securing the Govern-
ment sanction 1s 6,000 cars a year.
2. Automobile manufacturing companies to be
sanctioned under the law should be companies in which
a majority of deciding votes are controlled by Japanese
subjects or corporation organized by Japanese subjects
under Japanese law.
3. Automobile manufacturing companies organized
under this law shall be exempt from the business profit
tax, the income tax and other local taxes during a
specified period.
4. Automobiles companies sanctioned under this
law may issue debentures in amounts greater than those
designated by the Commercial Code.
5. The import of certain automobile parts or
machinery may be exempted from import tariff.
May Prevent Imports.
6. The Government may issue to automobile
companies formed under this law orders based on
national defence necessities.
7. According to public necessity the Government
may order automobile companies to alter prices on
equipment.
8. For protecting the domestic automobile industry,
the Government may take necessary steps to prevent the
import of foreign cars.
9. Those who were manufacturing automobiles on
August 9, 1935, will be regarded as being sanctioned
by the law.
By

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
By the last item, the increased entrance of
foreign capital in the motor car industry beyond the
amount standing on August 9, 1935, is prevented.
The Japanese Diet was dissolved on January 21, 1936,
for a general election, and possible enactment of the pro-
posed legislation is necessarily postponed thereby.
Several points with regard to the proposed legislation
should be noted. The proposal has to do with "mass cars,"
an expression which has been coined for this purpose and
which means to signify automobiles manufactured on a mass
production basis for general use; the proposed restrictions
would not affect automobiles in the luxury class. The
immediate purpose of the letter of August 9 from the Depart-
ment of Commerce and Industry to the automobile industry was
to serve as an official warning which would thereby lend a
tone of justification to terms of the legislation (which
could not immediately be passed because the Diet was not
sitting) making its terms retroactive to August 9. The
plants and rates of production of Ford and Chevrolet in
Japan as of August 9, 1935, as the limitation beyond which
those companies could not legally expand after the enactment
of the proposed legislation. Ford's interest in acquiring a
large tract of land for expansion of the Yokohama plant was
the immediate stimulus to the issue of the official letter
of August 9. It should also be noted that the proposed
legislation leaves considerable latitude in its administra-
tion

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
tion on account of lack of definition of how the licensing
system would actually be administered -- the familiar point
of uncertainty in so much of Japan's legislation.
Although the Japanese Government has not supplied the
American Government with any definitive statement of what
legislation is being considered for the regulation of the
automobile manufacturing industry in Japan, the above
paragraphs give a fairly specific idea of certain proposals,
and it is these proposals which the term "proposed automobile
legislation" is used to mean in this memorandum.
II. Would enforcement of the proposed automobile legislation
conflict with treaty rights of the United States?
Legislation proposed in Japan would permit expansion of
plant and output, in the production of automobiles, by
licensed companies only. Such licensed companies would have
to be of majority Japanese ownership. The present American
automobile manufacturers operating in Japan would be limited
to their plant and rate of output as of August 9, 1935.
Would the enforcement of such legislation violate American
treaty rights?
If the enforcement of the proposed legislation would
violate American treaty rights, the violation would be of
either article I or article VII of the 19ll Treaty of
Commerce and Navigation between the United States and Japan.
That treaty was concluded in the English language and the
text of those articles is as follows:
Article I.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
Article I.
The citizens or subjects of each of the High
Contracting Parties shall have liberty to enter,
travel and reside in the territories of the other
to carry on trade, wholesale and retail, to own or
lease and occupy houses, manufactories, warehouses
and shops, to employ agents of their choice, to
lease land for residential and commercial purposes,
and generally to do anything incident to or necessaary
for trade upon the same terms as native citizens or
subjects, submitting themselves to the laws and reg-
ulations there established.
They shall not be compelled, under any pretext
whatever, to pay any charges or taxes other or higher
than those that are or may be paid by native citizens
or subjects.
The citizens or subjects of each of the High
Contracting Parties shall receive, in the territories
of the other, the most constant protection and security
for their persons and property, and shall enjoy in
this respect the same rights and privileges as are or
may be granted to native citizens or subjects, on
their submitting themselves to the conditions imposed
upon the native citizens or subjects.
Article VII.
Limited liability and other companies and associ-
ations, commercial, industrial, and financial, already
or hereafter to be organized in accordance with the
laws of either High Contracting Party and domiciled in
the territories of such Party, are authorized, in the
territories of the other, to exercise their rights and
appear in the courts either as plaintiffs or defendants,
subject to the laws of such other Party.
The foregoing stipulation has no bearing upon the
question whether a company or association organized in
one of the two countries will or will not be permitted
to transact its business or industry in the other,
this permission remaining always subject to the laws
and regulations enacted or established in the respec-
tive countries or in any part thereof.
It

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
It is apparent from article VII that, although a company
incorporated in the United States has the right to appear in
the Japanese courts, the laws and regulations of Japan may
without violating the treaty refuse that company permission
to transact business or industry in Japan.
The assembling of automobiles in Japan by American
interests is, however, performed through the operation of
corporations established under Japanese law. The corpora-
tions are Japanese and their ownership is American, chiefly
or entirely. Their rights are therefore not governed by
article VII of the treaty, and a study of article I is
necessary.
Close observation of the punctuation of the first
sentence of article I indicates that it can be read in
several ways, one of which is:
The citizens or subjects of each of the High
Contracting Parties shall have liberty
to enter, travel and reside in the
territories of the other to carry
on trade, wholesale and retail,
to own or lease and occupy houses,
manufactories, warehouses and shops,
to employ agents of their choice,
to lease land for residential and
commercial purposes,
and generally to do anything incident to
or necessary for trade upon the same
terms as native citizens or subjects,
submitting themselves to the laws and
regulations there established.
It

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
It should be particularly observed that the words "to carry
on trade, wholesale and retail" are not preceded by a comma
and therefore do not constitute one of the coordinate clauses
of the statement but simply modify the clause "to enter,
travel and reside in the territories of the other."
It should not be overlooked that a close reading of the
first sentence of article I also permits the following some-
what more restricted meaning:
The citizens or subjects of each of the High
Contracting Parties shali have liberty
to enter, travel and reside in the
territories of the other
to carry on trade, wholesale
and retail,
to own or lease and occupy
houses, manufactories, ware-
houses and shops,
to employ agents of their
choice,
to lease land for residential
and commercial purposes,
and generally to do anything
incident to or necessary for
trade upon the same terms as
native citizens or subjects,
submitting themselves to the
laws and regulations there
established.
The definitive text of the treaty is in the English
language but it is worth noting how this particular sentence
has been officially rendered in Japanese by the Japanese
Foreign Office in a collection of treaties to which Japan is
a

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
a party, published in l925. This standard publication of
the Japanese Government publishes the English text of the
treaty and in a parallel column a rendering in Japanese.
The following is an English translation of the first sentence
of article I as rendered in Japanese in this official publica-
tion:
[Translation from Japanese.]
The subjects or citizens of each of the High
Contracting Parties, submitting themselves to the
laws and regulations established in the territories
of the other, shall, upon the same terms as native
subjects or citizens, have liberty
to enter, travel, and reside in the
territories of the other,
.to carry on trade, wholesale and retail,
to own or lease houses, manufactories,
warehouses, and shops, and make use of
them,
to employ agents of their choice,
to lease land for residential and
commercial purposes,
and otherwise generally to do anything
incident to or necessary for trade.
It is to be observed that in this rendering the Japanese
Foreign Office has ignored the above-mentioned comma and
has given the sentence its widest possible scope.
Sentences in article I which have to do with taxes and
compulsory military service are not in point in the present
matter. The remaining sentence of article I states as
follows:
The

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-11 -
The citizens or subjects of each of the High
Contracting Parties shall receive, in the territories
of the other, the most constant protection and security
for their persons and property, and shall enjoy in this
respect the same rights and privileges as are or may be
granted to native citizens or subjects, on their sub-
mitting themselves to the conditions imposed upon the
native citizens or subjects.
Although this sentence might conceivably be invoked by the
United States in support of a request for the protection of
the property of General Motors Japan and the Ford Motor
Company of Japan acquired in accordance with conditions
imposed upon native subjects, it is thought that the first
sentence of article I is more inclusive.
Narrowing down the subject of this inquiry, the present
question is whether or not the enforcement of the proposed
automobile legislation in Japan would violate terms of the
treaty which state (in their strictest reading) that the
citizens of the United States shall have liberty to reside
in Japan to own or lease and occupy manufactories and gen-
erally to do anything incident to or necessary for trade upon
the same terms as native subjects, submitting themselves to
the laws and regulations of Japan. American citizens have
the right to reside in Japan to own and occupy manufactories
and have the right to reside in Japan generally to do any-
thing incident to or necessary for trade upon the same terms
as native subjects, submitting themselves to the laws and
regulations of Japan. It is conceivable, speaking only in
terms of grammar, that the right to reside in Japan to do
anything incident to trade upon the same terms as native
subjects

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
subjects might be interpreted as a right of residence when
the purpose is trade on equal terms, and not as a right to
reside and trade on equal terms; but the Japanese Government
has not taken this position and is not likely to do so.
Entirely plausible, however, is the interpretation that the
first sentence of article I gives American citizens the right
to reside in Japan and participate in trade without handicap,
but that it does not give American citizens residing in the
United States the right to control an enterprise in Japan.
The possibility that the Japanese Government will adopt this
interpretation should not be overlooked. In the conversa-
tion of January 27, 1936, between the Japanese Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs and the Counselor the Vice Minister
spoke of absence of discrimination between Japanese subjects
and foreigners resident in Japan. This apparent distinction
may have been deliberate.
If American citizens have rights which would permit
them to assemble automobiles in Japan, does the 19ll treaty
give American citizens rights which would permit them to
assemble automobiles in Japan through the agency of
companies established under Japanese law? In a memorandum
of February 13, 1936, the Legal Adviser stated that 1f it
1s contended that article I of the treaty of l9ll does not
give American nationals the right to form corporations in
Japan

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
- 13 -
Japan for the purpose of carrying on business in that
country then it would be possible to nullify the pro-
visions of the treaty merely by requiring that all business
must be transacted through corporations or by limiting or
denying the right of aliens to participate in their for-
mation and operations. The attitude of the American
Government should be that the Japanese Government has the
right to provide such restrictions as it wishes for the
automobile industry of Japan and if it so desires may
enact a law stating that Japanese subjects have the right
to own a majority of the stock of companies licensed in
the industry, but that the citizens of the United States
(which enjoy a treaty guaranteeing to American citizens
in such trade the same terms as native subjects, sub-
mitting themselves to the laws and regulations) therefore
automatically acquire under such legislation the right to
own a majority of the stock of compenies licensed in the
production of automobiles.
On January 8 the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
observed in conversation with the Counselor of the Embassy
that the treaty of 19ll does not provide for rights of
manufacture. This was reported in the Embassy's telegram
No. 6, January 9, 1936, 11 a.m., and the Department replied
in telegram No. 14, January 31, 1936, 11 a.m., to the
Embassy stating"For your information the Department considers
that the use of the word 'manufactories' in its context in
articles

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
articles I and II of the treaty confers on American
citizens the indisputable right to manufacture on terms of
complete equality with Japanese subjects." The Department's
contention in this respect would appear to be entirely
sound. The first article of the treaty specifically speaks
of the ownership, leasing and occupying of manufactories,
and article II specifically mentions manufactories in con-
nection with the guarantee of respect of premises. There
can be no reasonable doubt that the intention of the treaty
was to include the right of manufacture among the rights
provided; and it is further to be noted that in the Japanese
rendering of the first sentence of article I (previously
mentioned on page 10) the right of manufacture is more
liberally set forth than in the original treaty.
It would be difficult to build up a case to show that,
in spite of the mention of manufactories in article I and
article II of the l9ll treaty, the actual right of manu-
facture was not contemplated.
Several articles in Japanese newspapers discussing the
proposed automobile legislation have taken the position that
such an enactment is necessary for national defense. It is
possible that the Foreign Office, after further study of the
treaty and after noting the legal difficulty of holding either
that article I is not applicable or that the right of manu-
facture is not included, will also conclude that the best
means of meeting any protest by the American Government
against

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
against the proposed automobile legislation is to justify
the legislation on the grounds of being necessary for
national defense. Three refutations of this argument
suggest themselves. First, commercial projects which play
a part in national defense are not exempted from the pro-
visions of the l9ll treaty which admit American citizens
to business in Japan on equal terms with Japanese subjects.
Second, the proposed automobile legislation is not necessary
to national defense. Even admitting that the automobile
industry be an industry essential to national defense, all
essential national defense considerations are met by the
location of the industry within Japan under conditions
enforced equally against Japanese subjects and American
citizens. It can be briefly observed in passing that the
law regulating the petroleum industry in Japan contains
provisions designed for the national defense but these
provisions are applicable equally to native and foreign
companies. Third, the last sentence of article I states
that American citizens shall be exempt in Japan "from all
forced loans or military exactions or contributions"; and
a disability imposed for reasons of national defense upon
American participants in the automobile industry in Japan
is a military exaction, consequently violative of the
treaty.
III.Would

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
-16 -
III. Would treaty rights of American interests in the
Ford Motor Company of Japan and General Motors Japan be
violated by enforcement of the proposed legislation?
General Motors Japan and the Ford Motor Company of Japan
have not taken the Department into confidence, and the owner-
ship of those two companies is not definitely known; but it is
generally assumed that a majority of the stock of the Ford
Motor Company of Japan is owned by the Ford Motor Company, a
Delaware'corporation, and that a majority of the stock of
General Motors Japan is owned by General Motors Corporation,
a Delaware corporation. If this assumption is correct the
Department should clearly bear in mind, in arriving at its
attitude toward the proposed automobile legislation in Japan,
that, under article VII of the treaty of 19ll between the
United States and Japan, the question whether or not a company
or association organized in the United States will be permitted
to transact its business or industry in Japan remains always
subject to the laws and regulations enacted in Japan. So far
as the proposed automobile legislation limits corporations
organized in the United States the treaty of 19ll is not
violated.
It is probable that it is the enforcement of legislation
contrary to a treaty and not the enactment of legislation con-
trary to a treaty which constitutes the violation of a treaty.
The enactment of the proposed automobile legislation would in
itself not constitute violation of the treaty of l9ll. Further-
more,

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
more, if the enacted legislation were enforced only against
corporations organized in the United States, it could be con-
tended that the treaty would not have been violated.
IV. What American interests would be affected by the proposed
automobile legislation in Japan?
The Ford Motor Company of Japan and General Motors Japan,
both of which are corporations established in accordance with
Japanese law and presumably owned by American corporations, own
and operate plants in Japan for the assembling of automobiles.
The plants of these two companies operate with American capital
and technical skill and Japanese labor. Some of the parts for
the automobiles assembled are manufactured in the United
States and imported into Japan by these corporations and some
of the parts are manufactured in Japan (chiefly or wholly by
other companies which are Japanese).
It is not impossible that, with the expansion of the Japa-
nese demand for automobiles, other American automobile manu-
facturers may later be interested in assembling in Japan. The
possible future interests of such other companies should
therefore not be overlooked in determining policy at the
present time.
The demand for automobiles in Japan is already of con-
siderable proportions and is expected to increase in later
years. In telegram No. 49, February 10, 1936, the Commercial
Attaché at Tokyo stated that official reports show that at
the end of October 1935 there were 134,859 motor vehicles in the
Japanese

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
-18 -
Japanese Empire exclusive of official vehicles; that of this
total 48,135 were automobile trucks; and that the total of
motor vehicles in the Japanese Empire increased by 13,667 in
the ten-month period January 1 to October 31, 1935. This
figure of 13,667 vehicles increase should not, however, be
taken as the figure representing the number of vehicles sold
in the same period. The market of course absorbed in addition
the number of automobiles required to replace those no longer
serviceable. In Japan the number of worn out vehicles is large
in comparison to the total number of vehicles in use beoause
most passenger automobiles are used as taxis which operate at
a high daily mileage and therefore have a relatively short life.
In the JAPAN ADVERTISER for November 3, 1935, the financial
editor of that newspaper, in an article on the automobile
industry, interpolated an estimate of the annual demand for
automobiles in Japan, fixing the figure at 34,000 vehicles.
It is probable that the financial editor had grounds for this
estimate, and it is further probable that he had consulted
General Motors Japan and the Ford Motor Company of Japan in
arriving at the figure.
Neither Ford nor General Motors have informed the
Department of the volume of their current production in Japan
but would probably be willing to divulge this information in
confidence if desired by the Department.
The retail price in Japan of 30,000 automobiles of
Chevrolet and Ford type at current prices is approximately
one hundred million yen.
v.What

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
- 19 -
V. What should be the policy of the United States with
regard to the proposed automobile legislation?
In 1nstruction No. 882, November 18, 1935, to the Embassy
at Tokyo the Department referred to the proposed legislation
and to article I of the l9ll treaty and concluded by stating:
The Department authorizes you to discuss orally
and informally with the Foreign Office the proposed
legislation and to inform the Foreign Office that in
the opinion of this Government the enactment and en-
forcement of the proposed law would be a clear viola-
tion of the rights to which American nationals engaged
in the business of manufacturing and distributing
motor vehicles in Japan are entitled by the treaty
article under reference.
Shortly after his return to Tokyo the Ambassador telegraphed
[No. 247, December 19, 5 p.m.] reporting the fact of current
negotiations between General Motors and Japanese interests in
regard to future manufacture in Japan, and the Ambassador
stated:
It is my opinion that this plan of General
Motors, even if carried into effect, will not
seriously weaken our basic treaty position and
therefore I propose that the Embassy first take up
the matter informally with Kurusu in an exploratory
way, requesting information in regard to details of
the draft legislation and stating that our Government
might consider such legislation as violative of
treaty rights.
The Department reiterated its above-quoted instruction of
November 18 by again stating [telegram No. 202, December 24,
1 p.m.] that "the Department inclines to the opinion that, if
the proposed legislation conforms to the Embassy's l440,
August 22, an oral statement as authorized in the final para-
graph of the Department's 882, November 18, would be desirable."
on

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
On January 8, 1936, the Counselor of the Embassy called
on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs and advised him orally
and informally of the American Government's attitude as set
forth in the final paragraph of the instruction of November 18
[Tokyo's telegram No. 6, January 9, 11 a.m.]. In Tokyo's tele-
grams No. 6, January 9, 11 a.m. and No. 22, February 1, noon,
the Embassy reported informal comments by the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs, the first to the effect that the treaty of 19ll
does not provide for rights of manufacture and the second to the
effect that article I applies to individuals and not to
corporations.
In reference to the attitude to be taken by the American
Government with regard to the proposed automobile legislation
in Japan the Economic Adviser has stated that he does not be-
lieve it to be to the American advantage to facilitate American
capital and enterprise to develop the automobile industry in
Japan. He inclines to the contrary opinion. It should never-
theless be borne in mind that economic advantage to American
exporters is not the only consideration involved in the question
of the American policy to be adopted toward the proposed auto-
mobile legislation. It is perhaps not likely that protests
by the United States Government will prevent Japanese measures
which will finally bring the automobile industry in Japan into
the control of Japanese interests, and it is probable that the
proposed legislation represents a determination of those in
control of the Japanese Government to bring under Japanese
ownership

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
-21 -
ownership the automobile industry of Japan, by means legal
or illegal (the Embassy has reported, in despatch No. 1659,
February 5, 1936, that the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
said that the Foreign Office is not in position to state to
other Departments that there is a treaty obligation which would
stand in the way of the proposed legislation); but a policy of
protest is nevertheless warranted on other grounds.
In the second section of this memorandum the conclusion
was reached that the proposed automobile legislation conflicts
with American treaty rights. The American Government has a
permanent interest in the principle of the conduct of inter-
national relations in accord with treaties and should not
ignore an unsound effort by the Japanese Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs legally to justify the proposed legislation;
and if the Japanese Government persists in the matter, the
Japanese interpretation of article I should, by communication
with the Foreign Office, be made unmistakably clear, to the
end that, as opportunity offers, the United States may
reciprocally interpret its obligations under treaties with
Japan in a similarly limited sense.
The courts in the United States [instruction No. 882,
November 18, 1935, to the Embassy at Tokyo, and also the
Legal Adviser's memorandum of February 13, 1936] have con-
strued article I of the l9ll treaty in a broad sense highly
favorable to Japanese; and if the Japanese Government is to
arrive at an interpretation of article I which is less
favorable

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.99)
-22 -
favorable to American citizens, it should be the aim of the
American Government to effect an exchange of notes with the
Japanese Government which might be cited in American courts
as evidence of the restricted Japanese interpretation of the
treaty.
Continued protest should be the policy of the American
Government notwithstanding the following considerations:
(1) The proposed automobile legislation, however
threatening, might perhaps not be enforced to the detriment
of American interests for some years to come. The proposal
would operate through a system of licenses which is permissive
and not mandatory upon the Jananese administrative officials,
and it is entirely conceivable that difficulties encountered
in developing the Japanese industry would result in operation
by the Ford Motor Company of Japan and by General Motors
Japan for a period of years practically without effective
limitation.
(2) Protest against the proposed legislation might
cause the Japanese Government to abandon its present project
and meet the desires of prospective Japanese automobile manu-
facturers by limitations which do not conflict with American
treaty rights and which would nevertheless be more damaging
to American automobile interests. The proposed legislation
might be abandoned and a much higher import tariff imposed
upon automobile parts. Pressure of protest might cause the
Japanese Government to re-study article I of the 19ll treaty
and

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
and come to the conclusion (mentioned in section II of this
memorandum) that American citizens who do not reside in
Japan may legally be excluded from the ownership of Japanese
corporations such as the Ford Motor Company of Japan and
General Motors Japan. Other legal means of handicapping the
companies might be devised. Or the Japanese Government might
choose abrogation. Article XVII of the treaty of 19ll provides
that it continues operative until six months from the date on
which either party shall have given notice of its intention
to terminate the treaty.
(3) Neither the Ford Motor Company, Ford Motor
Company of Japan, General Motors Corporation, nor General
Motors Japan has asked the Department that the American
Government protest the proposed legislation. It is probable
that these companies foresee practical difficulties on the part
of the Japanese before the actual interests of the companies
can be substantially damaged, and it is possible that the
American corporations (Ford Motor Company and General Motors
Corporation) are conscious of the fact that they themselves,
as foreign corporations, are in a vulnerable position. It is
also known that General Motors Japan is considering selling
out to Japanese interests, and its interest in protesting the
proposed automobile legislation is possibly removed by this
faot.
The

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.97)
-24 -
The protest against the proposed automobile legislation
in Japan which the Government of the United States is in
position to make is a protest in defense of the right of
American citizens in general and is not a protest in defense
of the particular American corporations which are presumed
to control the companies now manufacturing American auto-
mobiles in Japan. Assuming the enactment of the proposed
automobile legislation, the American Government will be
able to make out a strong treaty case for the rights of
American citizens resident in Japan to own controlling
shares in companies established under Japanese law to manu-
facture automobiles in Japan, and the American Government
will still be able to make out a treaty case of sorts for
the right of American citizens not resident in Japan to own
controlling shares in companies established under Japanese
law to manufacture automobiles in Japan; but the American
Government will not be able to make out a treaty case for the
right of American corporations to own controlling shares in
companies established under Japanese law to manufacture
automobiles in Japan. Protests must be on the basis of the
rights of American citizens and not on the basis of the
rights of American corporations.

Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1659,

matter is a conversation of January 27
between the Counselor of the Embassy and the
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs with re-
gard to the proposed automobile legislation
in Japan.
The enclosure to the despatch, which
is a memorandum of the conversation,
should be read with care. The Counselor
cites as a direct quotation from the Vice
Minister a long passage commenting on the
meaning of article I of the l9ll treaty
between Japan and the United States, with
reference to the proposed legislation.
The Vice Minister anparently states that
(1) the proposed legislation
will not permit individuals to en-
gage in the automobile manufactur-
ing industry, and in this respect
there will be no discrimination be-
tween Japanese subjects and
foreigners,
(2) companies incorporated in
the United States cannot, in view
of article VII of the treaty, object
to being excluded from the industry
by the requirement that the licensing
of

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of companies in the automobile
industry will be restricted to
companies established under Japanese
law,
(3) as a matter of administra-

to determine the conditions under
which companies established under
Japanese law may be permitted to go
into the industry, and
(4) the Ford Motor Company of
Japan and General Motors Japan are
companies established under Japanese
law.
COMMENT:
In his observations the Vice Minister
makes several statements which are true
and one which cannot be admitted, and he
ignores a fundamental question with re-
gard to the proposed legislation. It can-
not be admitted that as a matter of
administrative convenience Japanhas the
right to determine the conditions under
which companies established under Japanese
law may be permitted to go into the
automobile industry if this right means
also that Japan may exclude American
citizens from majority ownership of Japa-
nese corporations established to manu-
facture automobiles when such American
citizens comply with all other provisions
of the Japanese law. The snecific question
which the Vice Minister ignores is whether
or not the Japanese Government will
interpret article I of the 19ll treaty as
permitting

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.96)
-3 -
mitting the Japanese Government to
kclude American citizens from the right
of majority ownership of Japanese corpora-
tions for the manufacture of automobiles.
In my opinion the attitude of the
American Government on this question should
be made clear by a telegram in accordance
with the attached draft.

opPARMEt
人
Standard Size Cars
On page 4l of the confidential report referred
to above is given a memorandum of a conversation with
Mr. S. Yamamoto, then Managing Director of the Nissan
Jidosha Kabushiki Kaisha, in which he stated that re-
presentatives were then in the United States arranging
for the purchase of machine tools to produce a standard
sized car. The arrangements were made with the Graham-
Paige Company. Approximately $3,000,000 worth of
F/FG
machine

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
machine tools and dyes were purchased and American
technicians were employed to set up and operate the
new plant and train the Japanese personnel.
On May 29 to 31 inclusive, the Nissan Jidosha
Kabushiki Kaisha held an exhibition in Tokyo of its
new standard sized car, called the NISSAN. Enclosed
herewith are copies of the newspaper announcement of
the exhibition in which the specifications of the three
models (passenger car, truck chassis and bus chassis)
are given. The show was very well attended and re-
flected the general interest in what is claimed to
be a purely Japanese automobile.
The price of the car has not been announced by
the company but Japanese newspaper reports have pre-
dicted that the car would sell for Yen 5,Oo0 to Yen
5,500, although a Nissan official stated a year ago
that the new car would sell for Yen 3,000. The
Ford car sells for Yen 3,700 to Yen 4,000.
The actual chassis on display were assembled
from parts manufactured in the Graham-Paige plant in
Detroit. Parts for about 500 units were thus sent
out to facilitate the Nissan Company in getting its
cars on display. Although the American experts recently
employed by the Nissan Company to erect the equipment
and initiate production have been steadily at work it
is not believed that actual production of parts has
yet begun.
It is understood that originally the Nissan Company
did not intend to manufacture anything but trucks, but
that

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
that the Japanese Government would not give the company
permission to operate unless it would undertake the
manufacture of passenger vehicles also. As a result the
company has announced a complete line of truck, bus and
passenger models.
The NISSAN car is said to be a 1934 Graham-Paige
design which was not produced in any quantity by the
factory at that time, and which has been slightly re-
designed by the Graham-Paige engineers for the Nissan
Company. Nissan officials claim the car is the 1936
Graham-Paige model.
It is claimed that the Government will not give
cash subsidies to encourage the success of the NISSAN
venture, but that the car will be aided by substantial
import tariff protection, discriminatory taxation, and
other special treatment as against American proaucts.
The company has arranged for the manufacturing
rights in Japan, either on a royalty or lump sum basis,
of a number of American automobile parts, such as
clutches, differentials, universal joints, etc. As
none of these products had been patented in Japan the
company could have manufactured the parts without legal
difficulties. The company chose to make financial ar-
rangements, however, so as to get the benefit of
future improvements in those products.
Although the company announces that it will enter
the export field with the new car it is doubtful if
the company seriously hopes for such activity for several
years

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
years.
It is said that the labor costs in the NISSAN plant
are still very high due to the lack of experienced labor.
The same comment was made by a foreign technician in the
plant three years ago. It is expected that the labor
costs will be lowered when a sufficient force of skilled
laborers is trained to carry on the work.
Expert opinion indicates that there is serious
doubt that the present plans for the NIsSAN car will
succeed. In the first place the market for passenger
cars in Japan is too limited to support a first class
automotive industry. In the second place the company
is endeavoring to manufacture all special parts, such
as clutches, radiators, universal joints, etc., which
has not been found practicable even by the large manu-
facturers in the United States. A third handicap is
the necessity of importing nearly all special steels
used in the car, which adds considerably to the cost.
It is believed that the only practical basis on which
the Japanese can manufacture their own cars is under
some arrangement with an established American manufacturer,
such as the Nissan interests tried to arrange with the
General Motors Corporation two years ago. It is likely
that attempts will be made again to effect an arrange-
ment with an American company, though there is no
evidence of anything definitely planned as yet.
As the NISSAN car cannot be manufactured for such
a limited market as cheaply as American cars can be
imported

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
imported, even with the present import tariffs of 35% ad
valorem on assembled cars and automobile parts, the
Government will have to protect the Japanese product by
imposing much higher import tariffs. This will make
all automobiles more expensive and will presumably re-
tard any tendency toward increased use of motor vehicles.
The Government may also reduce the quotas limiting the
number of units the two American assembly plants (Ford
and General Motors) in Japan are allowed to build. It
may also force the taxicab owners, who represent 95% of
the buyers of passenger cars, to consolidate and reduce
the number of taxis in use. It is possible that the
Japanese authorities hope that restrictions limiting
the activities of the American assembly plants may
force one or the other to come to an agreement, eventually,
for Japanese ownership of Japanese manufacturing rights.
Datsun Midget Cars
Regarding the DATSUN midget car, which the company
manufactures, conflicting reports indicate its present
production as between 600 and 1500 units a month, mostly
delivery trucks which are replacing the three-wheeled
motorcycle delivery vans so widely used in Japan. Its
price has been increased by Yen l00 to around Yen 2100.
The company claims the demand for DATSUN cars is great-
er than its capacity to manufacture them.
Very respectfully yours,
Rraenue
Richard F. Boyce,
American Consul.

BY OIL COMPANY
Announcement of 1936 Bus
Transportation Awards Re-
veals Remarkable Record
With the announcement of the 1936
Bus Transportation  awards in the
United States recently, it is established
that the Texas Company, makers of
Texaco Oil products, have established
a new record for efficiency.
Here is Texaco's seven year record:
Since the Bus Transportation awards
were established in 1930, one or more
of the winners each year has used
Texaco products. Every gold award
winner has been a user of Texaco pro-
ducts-six in five years. In 1935, three
out of four winners were Texaco users.
In 1936, four out of five were Texaeo
users. Altogether, Texaco products
have been used by 14 winners in seven
years.
