MOTORDOM BOASTS
s, Passenger Car and Factory
NEW REFINEMENTS
Achievement of Well-balanoed
Relationship Hailed as Fea-
ture of Latest Automobiles
N.Y. Herald Tribune
Detailed study of the latest cars 
veals a multitude of desirable improve-
ments—improvements which as a group
were attainable only by painstaking
technical effort and solution of several
complex engineering problems.
Only engineers recognize fully how
easy it is to build into a car almost
any exaggerated characteristic along
any line. The difficult thing is to de-
sign a product in which there is a well
balanced relationship between the var-
ious aspects of performance, style,
comfort, economy, etc. Beyond a cer-
tain point no one of these characteris-
ties can be built into a car except at
the expense of something else.
It is in the achievement of this well
balanced relationship. together with a
notable improvement in practically
every desirable operating characteristic,
that the automotive engineers have
done an outstanding job in the latest
models. It may seem a bit contradic-
tory, perhaps. to talk about weight re-
duction, and larger bodies in the same
breath; to visualize fuel economy and
longer wheelbases as concomitant
trends; to tell of better performance
characteristics as well as less operat-
ing expense. But that is just what has
to be done if one is to describe what
has been accomplished in the 1937 cars.
These improved qualities have been
achieved through a myriad of detailed
refinements in every part of the auto-
mobile rather than by any single major
change either in position or function-
ing of the various units.
As was to be expected, the engineers
again have given serious attention to
the development of safety features—
features designed not only to protect
the occupants of the car in case of ac-
cident, but also to stimulate safe driving
and to make safe operation of the car
normal and natural. Particularly inter-
esting among the new safety features
which have been widely incorporated
this year are devices for keeping wind-
shields clear of frost and snow. This
defrosting equipment consists of warm
air blown from the car heater through
vents in the dashboard to the wind-
shield. The arrangement of the vents
cars, according to state ments by the management. Photos at top, left to right, illustrate the general
differs somewhat from car to car.
Yekohama was equipped at a cost
over ten million yen and is reported so constructed that it

most universal on the new models and
should be a really important contribu-
tion to safe and comfortable winter

THE JAPAN. -ADVERTISER, TO
SOCONY-VACUUM OIL
Activities of World Transportati
REPORT FAVORABLE
1936 Net Income Listed at
$1.38 Per Share, Compared
With 72 Cents for Last Year
Net income of Socony-Vacuum Oil
Co., in 1936 was $42.909,363, or $1.38 a
share. This compared with 72 cents a
share in 1935.
John A. Brown, president, in his
report to stockholders said that do-
mestic and foreign sales of products
(excluding companies 50 per cent own-
ed) totaled 113,260,000 barrels, a gain
of 11.9 per cent over 1935. The volume
of business abroad, he said, showed
satisfactory increases.
Capital expenditures in 1936, exclud-
ing companies 50 per cent owned, totaled
$76,500,000, of which $43.500.000 was for
crude oil development and properties,
Mr. Brown declared.
Net fixed assets increased $24.202,464
and miscellaneous investments and ad-
vances increased $6,014.697. Mr. Brown
added:
"Cash and marketable securities de-
creased $15,681,933 in 1936. but the in-
creases in receivables nad a small in-
crease in inventories resulted in a net
decline in current assets of only $9.-
787,907. Current liabilities were up
10,779,211, caused by a great increase
in taxes payable at the end of the year,
larger accounts payable, and substantial
purchase obligations. mostly due to
purchases of properties for crude oil
production operations.
Gross crude oil production in the
United States was 48,203.362 barrels
for 1936, an increase of 9 per cent over
the year 1935.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 25, 1937.
The manager of the Ford Motor Company
of Japan informed the Embassy that in re-
sponse to the market's demand the company
had imported about 3,600 assembled units over
and above the quota of 12,360 which was
allowed the company under the Automobile
Industry Control Law. In addition, the com-
pany has sent an order to Detroit for 3,000
more units.
Apparently the authorities are aware of,
and do not oppose, such importations. How-
ever, in the opinion of the manager, as soon
as the present military emergency is over,
the authorities will again apply the policy
of restrioting and hampering the company's
operations.

Mr. Benjamin Kopf, Manager of the Ford Motor Company of
Japan, Limited, called at the Embassy this morning and

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 2517 dated July 28, 1937,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
July 28, 1937.
Mr. Benjamin Kopf, Manager Ford Company
of Japan, Limited, and Mir. McGurk,
First Secretary of the Embassy.
Mr. Kopf called this morning by request in order that
the Department's views set forth in telegram No. ll8 of
July 20, 5 p.m., might be explained to him. The position
of the Ford Company and the Department's position were
reviewed in the light of the above-mentioned telegram and
the Embassy's despatch No. 2394 of April 30, 1937. Mr. Kopf
said that he appreciated all that the Department and the
Embassy had done in the premises and that he thought it
would be useless again to take up with the Japanese auth-
orities the Company's situation.
Mr. Kopf went on to say that the Company had imported
about 3,6o0 complete units over and above the quota of
12,360 units which they were allowed to assemble in Japan
under the Automobile Industry Control Law. He said that
the number of units imported was in no way contrary to the
restriction on assembled cars and that the authoritie s were
well aware that the complete units had been imported.
Mr. Kopf said that he had recently been approached by
an official of the Department of Commerce and Industry
who inquired whether the Ford Company could double its
present production capacity of l0o units per eight-hour
day. Mr. Kopf said that he replied that by working two
eight-hour shifts per day 2o0 units per day could be produced
but he informed the official of the Department of Commerce
and Industry that not more than loo bodies per day could be
produced

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
produced in Japan at the present time. Mr. Kopf said that
he believed that the inquiry was made on behalf of the
military authorities and he suspected that if the Company
were requested to increase its capacity to 2oo units per day
that the railway shops would be directed to supply suffi-
cient bodies for the extra lo0 units.
Mr. Kopf informed me in confidence that he had a few
days ago sent an order to the factory at Detroit for 30oo
additional units. He said that he could very easily dispose
of them to those whose cars had a lreaay been commandeered by
the military authorities and that in any case he was certain
that in the event he couid not sell them as replacements to
those whose cars had been commandeered the military authori-
ties would take them.
Mr. Kopf said that while for the present the situation
of the Company was satisfactory he had no doubt that once
the present incident was settled the authorities would again
apply t he policy of hindering and restricting the Company's
operations.
JFMCG:C

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 17, 1938.
Reference Yokohama's Despatch
No. 345, December 13, 1937, entitled
"Automotive Industry of Japan".
in, maton
The newe conveyed in this despatch
in regard to the Nissan automobile
venture in Japan is not new. The com-
pany is now making about twenty cars
a day, nearly all being trucks taken
by the Japanese military authorities.
It is interesting to note on page 3,
however, that the manager of the Ford
Motor Company of Japan recently told
Consul Boyce at Yokohama that Mr.
Aikawa "has realized that the Nissan,
and presumably Datsun, venture is a
failure and that he has lost interest
in it. The same informant states
that the Japanese Government also real-
izes the failure of the venture and
also the failure of the Government's
regulations restricting American auto-
mobile assembly plants in Japan. He
states that the Ford company has had
its best year of business this year
and that in a few days the Government
will announce new regulations liberal-
izing the control over the American
plants."

SIR
8
I have the honor to refer to previous reports upon
the automotive industry of Japan, particularly to the
confidential report accompanying Despatch No. 219, of
June 12, 1936, and to Despatch No. 308, of June 8, 1937.
An American representative of the Graham-Paige
automobile company of Detroit who has been employed in
the Yokohama plant of the Nissan Jidosha Kabushiki
Kaisha, engaged in setting up the machinery sold by
the Graham-Paige company, is returning to Detroit. He
supplied the following information.
NISSAN production.
JAN 27 1938
FL
The NISSAN is the standard sized car made from
plans and with equipment purchased from the Graham-
Paige Company of Detroit. As reported in the Despatch
of June 8, l937, referred to above the first few hundred
units were assembled from parts sent from the United
States
1

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
States by the Graham-Paige Company. Since then some
of its parts are still imported, such as the wheels
and rear axles. It manufactures its own engines. At
present the production of NISSAN cars is 20 units a
day. Nearly all are trucks and are taken by the Japanese
military authorities. Although the company claims that
the units have performed successfully in military service
it is not explained why efforts are not made to step up
production.
The American engineer stated that some departments
of the plant are ready for a production of 150 units a
day but that others can not produce more than 20 units,
because of lack of equipment.
The engineer says that the NISSAN motor is a 1927
model engine that the Graham-Paige company produced
until about 1934; that the machine equipment bought by
the NISSAN is the same equipment used by the Graham-
Paige plant since that time and that the equipment is
good for several years if properly cared for. He says
that any change in motor design would require new
equipment which would cost as much as that which they
have purchased.
Regarding skilled labor, the engineer says that
the Japanese company employs very young men as apprentices
and that the new men are quick to learn their work and are
satisfactory under the conditions prevailing. He says
that in the NISSAN plant there is an operator and an
assistant for every machine, whereas one operator, single-
handed, would handle three machines in an American plant.
Regarding metals used in construction of the NISSAN
the engineer says that the Japanese have changed some of
the

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.96)
-3 -
the materials used in the American model to materials
available here, but that it will probably always be
necessary to import a considerable quantity of special
steels.
Views of a Competitor of NISSAN JIDOSHA KABUSHIKI KAISHA
In a recent conversation with the manager of the
FORD MOTOR COMPANY OF JAPAN the manager stated that he
had reliable information that Mr. Yoshisuke Aikawa,
x
President of the NISSAN interests, has realized that the
NISSAN, and presumably DATSUN, venture is a failure and
that he has lost interest in it.
The same informant states that the Japanese Government
also realizes the failure of the venture and also the
failure of the Government's regulations restricting
American automobile assembly plants in Japan. He states
that the FORD company has had its best year of business
this year and that in a few days the Government will
announce new regulations liberalizing the control over
the American plants.
DATSUN Midget Cars
The production of the DATSUN midget cars is now
35 units a day. These are mostly delivery vans.

Reference a report entitled "Japan's
Automotive Industry and the China Incident"
prepared by Consul John M. Allison at Osaka
on March 23, 1939.
The first 8 pages of the report describe
the automotive situation in Japan prior to
1938. The most significant figures are those
on page 5 which indicate that in 1936 there
6,633 were small-sized cars of the Datsun
type),and cars assembled locally from imported
parts numbered 30,997. The Japanese produc-
tion for l936 represented a considerable
increase over production in l935 but most of
the inorease occurred in the small-sized cars.
Since 1936 the Ford Motor Company and
General Motors Company, who have assembly
plants in Japan, have been put on a production
quota. The Ford Company has been allowed a
quota of 12,360 units a year while General
Motors has been allowed 9,470 units a year.
The statistics on page 7 are interesting
in that they show that imports from Germany,
although small in comparison with the total,
increased considerably between 1934 and 1937.
In a latter section the report indicates that
efforts were being made to stimulate especially
imports from Germany.
In

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
In describing the situation during and
since 1938 (beginning on page 8) Mr. Allison
deals principally with hearsay information
(which is about all that is available) and
in my opinion gives too much credence to
exaggerated reports of Japanese production.
The Government's three-year plan calls for
a production of 80,000 motor cars by 1941
and by that time Japan will be entirely self-
sufficient. It is also reported that the
combined production of the two chief Japanese
companies, Toyoda and Nissan, reached almost
20,000 units of which 15,000 units were
passenger cars. It seems to me that all of
these figures are more in the realm of
fantasy than of fact. At the same time I
believe that the report minimizes the
importance of imports to Japan's supply of
motor vehicles. The report mentions only
incidentally and at the end of the report
that imports of completed motor vehicles have
inoreased enormously since the beginning of
the conflict in China, although the produc-
tion allotment of General Motors and Ford
have not been increased and in fact have not
been allowed to produce up to the allotted
quotas.
The Japanese Government is endeavoring
to encourage the use of charcoal burning
vehicles.
The

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
The importation of passenger vehicles
has been out down drastically and most of the
motor vehicles imported are taken by the
military.
Mr. Allison is pessimistic in regard
to the future of American automobiles in
Japan.
The report under reference states
(page 23) that "if the American Government
wishes to continue its present policy of
discouraging the granting of credits or loans
by American firms ..... stronger measures
than moral suasion will be needed". This
opinion presumably arises principally out of
the faot that Japanese newspapers indicated
that the Ford Motor Company was selling a
large order of Ford motor cars to the
Manchuria Heavy Industries Company through
some sort of barter arrangement. (The
Department has since been informed that the
Ford Motor Company sells motor cars to Japan
and Manchuria only for cash).
Consul Boyce at Yokohama recently
submitted a report entitled "Japanese Auto-
motive Industry, 1937-1938", dated December
14, 1938. The divergenoe between the
interpretation in Consul Boyce's report and
that in Consul Allison's report is very
large. Consul Boyce emphasizes the fact that
the Japanese Army is to a large degree
dependent upon American automobiles and trucks
but

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
but the local industry is small and
ineffioient and depends to a great extent
upon imported parts. Consul Boyce has
nothing good to say about the Japanese
automotive industry and its prospects but he
is likewise pessimistic about the future of
American automobiles in Japan. In my opinion
Consul Boyce's interpretation is much more
realistio than that of Consul Allison and is
much more critical of data presented.te m.

INTRODUCTION
Since the outbreak of the present hostilities
in China, considerable pro ress has been shown in
the automotive industry in Japan. Native production
has more than tripled while at the same time restric-
tions placed upon the two American manuracturers who
maintain assembly plants in Japan have increased.
The authorities have recently announced a three year
plan which will aim at makin- Janan self-sufficient
in the automotive field, end at the same time the
local press has announced that the istribution of
Ford and Chevrolet cars will ce prohit ite. after the
1。
first half of 1939.
This report will aiscuss these chanres in Janan's
automotive industry as seen fron csara, the nation's
industrial center and the seat of one of the two
American asser.bl: plants anu comment upon their pos-
sible effect on American exports of autonotive pro-
ducts. There is no attenpt made to treet the subject
exhaustively, as uncer present conditions it is not
only impossible to obtain surricient inrormation but
also inadvisable to make an effort to do so. No
official statistics regarding production or imports
of automobiles and parts lhave been published since
the end of June 1937, ene the information iver telow
has been obtained fror locel press accounts end through
talks with persons engeged in the automotive trade.

SITUATION DURING AND SINCE 1938.
All information about the progress of the auto-
motive industry in Japan during the past year must
be based upon newspaper accounts and interviews with
persons engaged in the industry as no official
statistics

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
statistics have been published since the end of June
1937. The automotive industry is considered by the
Government as one of the key industries in connection
with national defense and actual figures are closely
guarded. However, it has been possible to obtain
enough information to make a close estimate of the
present condition of the industry and its future
prospects.
The Government has announced a three year plan
which calls for the prouuction in Janan of 80,000 motor
3.
cars by 1941,
and it is hoped that by that year Japan
will be entirely self-sufficient as regards eutomotive
production. According to a reliable source,
the
combined production of the two chief Japanese companies,
Toyoda and Nissan, during 1938, reached almost 20,000
units, some 15,000 units being trucks and the remainder
passenger cars. This same source believes that it is
quite possible that these same companies will produce
as many as 25,000 units durin 1939, and that within two
or three years at the most, Japanese companies will be
producing all the motor vehicles needed in this country.
At the same time that this expansion of native
production has been noted there have been increasing
restrictions placed upon the American factories in
Japan as well as a virtual cessation of imports of
American cars other than Ford and Chevrolet. As
stated above the local press announced on March l0th

FUTURE OF
AMERICAN AUTONOTIVE INPORTS INTO JAPAN
The value of American automotive exports to Japan
is not inconsiderable, amounting to more than United
States Do1lars 10,000,000 a year for 1936, 1937 and
1938. In 1936, the last year for which complete
American figures are available to this office, the
value of automotive exports to Japan amounted to
$11,609,344, according to the United States Department
of

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
of Commerce reports. This was nearly 5 percent of the
total American automotive exports of 239,147,123.
During the first eight months of l938, American auto-
motive exports to Japan totalled
$8,630,000 as compared
with 12,647,000 during the same period of 1937.
would therefore appear that while there was approximately
a 30 percent decrease in 193e over 1937, the value is
still substantial and any in ication that these exports
may continue to decrease will be a matter of some con-
cern to American manufacturers.
In assessing the probable future course of American
automotive exports to Japan there are several factors
which must be taken into consiieration. The rising
spirit of nationalism in Japan, brin in wi th it a
determination to develon a domestic autonotive industry
however unsound economically and technically, is perhans
the chief unfavorable factor in the outlook for American
cars. The urgent necessity durine this emergency period
to conserve the financial resources of the country which
has caused increasing restrictions to be nlaced upon
the granting of foreign exchange permits is the other
chief unfavorable fector.
. As stated above, the  ilitary
authorities recornize the superiority of the American
product anc in spite of the esire to foster a comestic
industry would probably continue to purchase A erican
makes for use in military operations, providin: financial
considerations did not enter the nicture. It is because
of this desire to have the high quality American product
and at the same time reluctance to part with additional
cash reserves, that Japanese official and semi-official
organizations

CONCLUSION
In conclusion it may be said that the effect of
the China Incident upon the automotive industry in
Japan has been to increase greatly the productive
capacity of the native Japanese manufacturers while at
the same time placing more and more restrictions upon
the importation of automobiles and parts for assembly
from the United States. It is probable that this
situation would have come about in any event but it
is certain that the advent of nostilities on the conti-
nent has accel erated the process. The lead which the
dome st ic

domestic manufacturers are now obtainin. over their
foreign, principally American, cennetitors will stand
them in good stead after the present hostilities have
ceased and it is believed it can sefely be seid that
the period of don ination of Janan's autonotive industry
by America is definitel: on the decline. Because of
the increased proauction in Jenen of eutomopile re-
placement parts for export, as ind icated above, it may
even be thet American menufacturers will in time lose
much of their presenttrade in such items with the
countries of Asia and perhans Latin merica. Particular-
ly in asia, where price is the d minatin~ factor in
making sales, if the Jenano are sble to produce a
cheeper article it will rind reeoy buyers even if the
quality is not up to that of the American original.
The Japenese are deternined to develop their own industry
and it is believed that, in the acsence of untovard
developments not now rcrseen, they will succeed.
